is constructed from
M_{0}\times I\simeq M\simeq\Cyl_{F_{i}}
by adding
(q+1)
-handles.

So all of these homology groups are free (over
\mathbb{Z}G
), and we equip them with the previously chosen bases. The long exact sequence

(3.4)

\displaystyle\cdots\rightarrow H_{q+2}(\Cyl_{F_{i}},W_{i})\rightarrow H_{q+1}(W_{i},N)\rightarrow

\displaystyle H_{q+1}(\Cyl_{F_{i}},N)\rightarrow H_{q+1}(\Cyl_{F_{i}},W_{i})\rightarrow H_{q}(W_{i},N)\rightarrow\cdots

is therefore isomorphic to the short exact sequence (
3.2
). If we regard it as a based free chain complex with vanishing homology, then by the sign conventions in the definition (see
[
45
, p. 365]
) its Whitehead torsion is
(-1)^{q}x_{i}
. The cobordism
W_{i}
is simple homotopy equivalent to
N
with a
(q+1)
-cell attached for each basis element of
L_{i}
, so

C_{*}(W_{i},N)\cong C_{q+1}(W_{i},N)\cong L_{i}\cong H_{q+1}(W_{i},N)\cong H_{*}(W_{i},N)

with the same choice of basis, therefore, again by the definition,
\tau(W_{i},N)=0
. Finally,
\Cyl_{F_{i}}\simeq_{s}M\simeq_{s}\Cyl_{g}
by
Proposition

2.30
, so
\tau(\Cyl_{F_{i}},N)=\tau(\Cyl_{g},N)
. Hence by
[
45
, Theorem 3.2]
we have

\tau(\Cyl_{g},N)=\tau(\Cyl_{F_{i}},W_{i})+(-1)^{q}x_{i},

which implies that

x_{1}-x_{0}=(-1)^{q+1}(\tau(\Cyl_{F_{1}},W_{1})-\tau(\Cyl_{F_{0}},W_{0})).

Next consider the triple
(\Cyl_{F_{i}},W_{i},M_{i})
. Since
W_{i}
can be obtained from
M_{i}\times I
by adding
k
trivial
q
-handles, we have
W_{i}\simeq_{s}M_{i}\vee(\bigvee^{k}S^{q})
. We choose the basis corresponding to the spheres in both
C_{*}(W_{i},M_{i})\cong C_{q}(W_{i},M_{i})\cong C_{q}(\bigvee^{k}S^{q})
and
H_{*}(W_{i},M_{i})\cong H_{q}(W_{i},M_{i})\cong H_{q}(\bigvee^{k}S^{q})
, so that
\tau(W_{i},M_{i})=0
. Since
f_{i}\colon M_{i}\rightarrow M
is a homotopy equivalence and
\Cyl_{F_{i}}\simeq_{s}M\simeq_{s}\Cyl_{F_{i}}
, we have
H_{*}(\Cyl_{F_{i}},M_{i})\cong H_{*}(\Cyl_{F_{i}},M_{i})=0
and
\tau(\Cyl_{F_{i}},M_{i})=\tau(\Cyl_{F_{i}},M_{i})=\tau(f_{i})
. In the homological long exact sequence of the triple
(\Cyl_{F_{i}},W_{i},M_{i})
all terms are trivial except for the isomorphism

(3.5)

0\rightarrow H_{q+1}(\Cyl_{F_{i}},W_{i})\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{q}(W_{i},M_{i})\rightarrow 0

We equipped
H_{q+1}(\Cyl_{F_{i}},W_{i})\cong L_{i}^{*}
with the dual of the basis of
L_{i}
. Since the handles added to
M_{i}\times I
to obtain
W_{i}
(determining the basis of
H_{q}(W_{i},M_{i})
) are the duals of the handles added to
N\times I
to obtain
W_{i}
(corresponding to the basis if
L_{i}
), the isomorphism (
3.5
) preserves the basis. Therefore the Whitehead torsion of the long exact sequence (
3.4
) vanishes. So by
[
45
, Theorem 3.2]
we have

\tau(f_{i})=\tau(\Cyl_{F_{i}},W_{i})
.
Therefore
x_{1}-x_{0}=(-1)^{q+1}(\tau(f_{1})-\tau(f_{0}))
, which implies that
\pi(x_{1}-x_{0})=\pi(\tau(f_{1})-\tau(f_{0}))
. Combining with (
3.3
), we have
\pi(\tau(f_{1})-\tau(f_{0}))=\pi(x_{1}-x_{0})=\psi(z)
.
Hence, as required, we have:

\displaystyle\widehat{\tau}([M_{0},f_{0}]^{z})

\displaystyle=\widehat{\tau}([M_{1},f_{1}])=\pi(\tau(f_{1}))

\displaystyle=\pi(\tau(f_{0}))+\pi(\tau(f_{1})-\tau(f_{0}))=\pi(\tau(f_{0}))+\pi(x_{1}-x_{0})

\displaystyle=\widehat{\tau}([M_{0},f_{0}])+\psi(z)=\widehat{\tau}([M_{0},f_{0}])^{z}.\qed

Proof of
Proposition

3.7
.

The first triangle commutes by
Proposition

3.6
combining with the fact that
\widehat{\tau}([M,\Id])=0
. The last triangle commutes by the definitions of
\sigma_{s}
and
\sigma_{h}
, see e.g.
[
52
, p.359–360]
. The map
\sigma_{h}
is by definition the map
\sigma_{s}
followed by the map
F\colon L_{n}^{s}(\mathbb{Z}G,w)\to L_{n}^{h}(\mathbb{Z}G,w)
that forgets bases.
So we need to prove that the square commutes. Let
[N,f]\in\mathcal{S}^{h}(M)
, then
\eta_{h}([N,f])\in\mathcal{N}(M)
is the normal bordism class of
f\colon N\rightarrow M
(with an appropriate bundle map), and
\widehat{\tau}([N,f])=\pi(\tau(f))
. We need to determine the image of these elements in
L^{s}_{n}(\mathbb{Z}G,w)
.

First assume that
n=2q
is even. Since
f
is
q
-connected,
\sigma_{s}(\eta_{h}([N,f]))
is defined as a form on
\ker(H_{q}(f))\cong H_{q+1}(\Cyl_{f},N)
, which is trivial (because
f
is a homotopy equivalence), and it is equipped with a certain stable basis. The stable basis of
H_{q+1}(\Cyl_{f},N)=0
is chosen such that with this choice
\tau(\Cyl_{f},N)=0
(where
H_{i}(\Cyl_{f},N)=0
is equipped with the canonical stable basis if
i\neq q+1
). If
H_{q+1}(\Cyl_{f},N)
were also equipped with the canonical stable basis, then the Whitehead torsion of
(\Cyl_{f},N)
would be equal to
\tau(f)
. Therefore the transition matrix between the chosen and the standard stable basis (when both are regarded as bases of
\mathbb{Z}G^{k}
for some
k\geq 0
) has Whitehead torsion
(-1)^{q}\tau(f)
. In
L^{s}_{n}(\mathbb{Z}G,w)
the same element
\sigma_{s}(\eta_{h}([N,f]))
is also represented by a standard hyperbolic form with a basis such that the transition matrix between the chosen and the standard basis has Whitehead torsion
(-1)^{q}\tau(f)
.

The image of
\pi(\tau(f))
in
L^{s}_{n}(\mathbb{Z}G,w)
is represented by a standard hyperbolic form with a basis with the property that if
x
denotes the Whitehead torsion of the transition matrix between the chosen and the standard basis, then
x\in\mathcal{J}_{n}(G,w)
and
\pi(x)=\pi(\tau(f))
(see
[
60
, p. 312]
). In particular, the representative of
\sigma_{s}(\eta_{h}([N,f]))
constructed above also represents
\varrho(\widehat{\tau}([N,f]))
, noting that
\pi((-1)^{q}\tau(f))=\pi(\tau(f))
, because
\widehat{H}^{n+1}(C_{2};\Wh(G,w))
is
2
-torsion. Hence the square commutes if
n
is even.

Next consider the case when
n=2q+1
is odd. Let
U
denote a tubular neighbourhood of a disjoint union of embeddings
S^{q}\rightarrow N
representing a generating set of
\ker(\pi_{q}(f))
and let
N_{0}=N\setminus\interior U
. We identify
H_{q}(\partial U)
with the standard hyperbolic form, then
H_{q+1}(U,\partial U)
(more precisely, its image under the boundary map) corresponds to the standard lagrangian. We can assume that
f\big|_{U}
is constant, so
f\big|_{N_{0}}
is a map of pairs
(N_{0},\partial U)\rightarrow(M,\ast)
. Then
\ker(H_{q+1}(N_{0},\partial U)\rightarrow H_{q+1}(M,\ast))\cong H_{q+2}(\Cyl_{f|_{N_{0}}},N_{0}\cup\Cyl_{f|_{\partial U}})
determines another lagrangian in
H_{q}(\partial U)
. We equip
H_{q}(\partial U)
and
H_{q+1}(U,\partial U)
with their standard bases, and
H_{q+2}(\Cyl_{f|_{N_{0}}},N_{0}\cup\Cyl_{f|_{\partial U}})
with a stable basis such that
\tau(\Cyl_{f|_{N_{0}}},N_{0}\cup\Cyl_{f|_{\partial U}})=0
. Then
\sigma_{s}(\eta_{h}([N,f]))
is represented by the formation
(H_{q}(\partial U);H_{q+1}(U,\partial U),H_{q+2}(\Cyl_{f|_{N_{0}}},N_{0}\cup\Cyl_{f|_{\partial U}}))
. Since
f
is a homotopy equivalence, we can take the empty generating set for
\ker(\pi_{q}(f))
. Then
H_{q}(\partial U)=0
, so we have the trivial formation, with the standard basis on the ambient form and on the first lagrangian. The stable basis on the second lagrangian,
H_{q+2}(\Cyl_{f},N)
, is chosen such that
\tau(\Cyl_{f},N)=0
. Therefore the transition matrix between this stable basis and the standard one has Whitehead torsion
(-1)^{q+1}\tau(f)
. In
L^{s}_{n}(\mathbb{Z}G,w)
the same element
\sigma_{s}(\eta_{h}([N,f]))
is also represented by a formation on the standard hyperbolic form given by the standard lagrangians, such that the ambient form and the first lagrangian are equipped with their standard bases, and for the second lagrangian the transition matrix between the chosen and the standard basis has Whitehead torsion
(-1)^{q+1}\tau(f)
.

The image of
\pi(\tau(f))
in
L^{s}_{n}(\mathbb{Z}G,w)
is represented by a formation on the standard hyperbolic form given by the standard lagrangians, such that the ambient form and the first lagrangian are equipped with their standard bases, and if
x
denotes the Whitehead torsion of the transition matrix between the chosen and the standard basis of the second lagrangian, then
x\in\mathcal{J}_{n}(G,w)
and
\pi(x)=\pi(\tau(f))
. Again we use that we can ignore signs because
\widehat{H}^{n+1}(C_{2};\Wh(G,w))
is
2
-torsion. So the representative of
\sigma_{s}(\eta_{h}([N,f]))
constructed above also represents
\varrho(\widehat{\tau}([N,f]))
, showing that the square also commutes if
n
is odd.
∎

Corollary 3.9
.

(a)

\image\widehat{\tau}=\varrho^{-1}(\image\sigma_{s})
.

(b)

\image\psi\subseteq\varrho^{-1}(\image\sigma_{s})
.

Proof.

First we prove (
a
). The commutativity of the square in
Proposition

3.7
implies that
\image\widehat{\tau}\subseteq\varrho^{-1}(\image\sigma_{s})
. For the other direction assume that
x\in\widehat{H}^{n+1}(C_{2};\Wh(G,w))
and
\varrho(x)=\sigma_{s}(y)
for some
y\in\mathcal{N}(M)
. Then
\sigma_{h}(y)=F\circ\sigma_{s}(y)=F\circ\varrho(x)=0
, so
y=\eta_{h}(v)
for some
v\in\mathcal{S}^{h}(M)
. Then
\varrho(x-\widehat{\tau}(v))=\varrho(x)-\sigma_{s}(\eta_{h}(v))=\varrho(x)-\sigma_{s}(y)=0
, so
x-\widehat{\tau}(v)=\psi(z)
for some
z\in L^{h}_{n+1}(\mathbb{Z}G,w)
. So by
Proposition

3.6

x=\widehat{\tau}(v)^{z}=\widehat{\tau}(v^{z})\in\image\widehat{\tau}
.

For (
b
), since
\Id_{M}
has vanishing surgery obstruction, we have
\image\psi=\ker\varrho\subseteq\varrho^{-1}(\image\sigma_{s})
.
∎

Corollary 3.10
.

Let
x\in\widehat{H}^{n+1}(C_{2};\Wh(G,w))
. If
x\in\image\psi
, then there is a
\CAT

n
-manifold
N
and a homotopy equivalence
f\colon N\to M
such that
\pi(\tau(f))=x
.

Proof.

By
Corollary

3.9
,
x=\widehat{\tau}([N,f])
for some
[N,f]\in\mathcal{S}^{h}(M)
.
∎

Remark 3.11
.

There is a common generalisation of the two realisation techniques used in
Section

3
.
Let
L_{n}^{s,\tau}(\mathbb{Z}G,w)
denote the surgery obstruction groups defined in
[
38
, Section 4]
. Similarly to
L_{n}^{s}(\mathbb{Z}G,w)
, the elements of
L_{n}^{s,\tau}(\mathbb{Z}G,w)
are represented by based nonsingular forms/formations, but, unlike the elements of
L_{n}^{s}(\mathbb{Z}G,w)
, they are not assumed to be simple. Thus there is a natural forgetful map
L_{n}^{s,\tau}(\mathbb{Z}G,w)\to L_{n}^{h}(\mathbb{Z}G,w)
and
L_{n}^{s}(\mathbb{Z}G,w)
is the kernel of a map
L_{n}^{s,\tau}(\mathbb{Z}G,w)\to\Wh(G,w)
(see
[
38
]
). There is also an action of
\Wh(G,w)
on
L_{n}^{s,\tau}(\mathbb{Z}G,w)
(which determines a map
\Wh(G,w)\to L_{n}^{s,\tau}(\mathbb{Z}G,w)
by acting on
0
) given by changing the basis; this action is transitive on the fibers of the
L_{n}^{s,\tau}(\mathbb{Z}G,w)\to L_{n}^{h}(\mathbb{Z}G,w)
.

Wall’s construction for realising elements of
L_{n}^{s}(\mathbb{Z}G,w)

[
72
]
can also be applied to
L_{n}^{s,\tau}
, but it will only produce homotopy equivalences, not simple homotopy equivalences. The Whitehead torsion of the resulting homotopy equivalence is given by the previously mentioned map
L_{n}^{s,\tau}(\mathbb{Z}G,w)\to\Wh(G,w)
, and in fact the image of this map is in
\mathcal{J}_{n}(G,w)
.

The realisation of
L_{n}^{h}(\mathbb{Z}G,w)
, and the map
W_{h}
, can be regarded as a special case of this construction, by first choosing a basis for the form/formation representing an element of
L_{n}^{h}(\mathbb{Z}G,w)
, equivalently, choosing a lift in
L_{n}^{s,\tau}(\mathbb{Z}G,w)
. As the choice of basis is not unique, the homotopy equivalence we obtain is not unique, and its Whitehead torsion (the value of
\widehat{\tau}
) is only well-defined in the quotient
\widehat{H}^{n+1}(C_{2};\Wh(G,w))
. The realisation part of the
s
-cobordism theorem is also a special case via the map
\Wh(G,w)\to L_{n}^{s,\tau}(\mathbb{Z}G,w)
. The Whitehead torsion of the homotopy equivalence induced by the
h
-cobordism we get is of course in
\mathcal{I}_{n}(G,w)
.

In the rest of this section we will consider the orientable (
w\equiv 1
) case, and omit
w
from the notation.

Proposition 3.12
.

Let
G_{m}\coloneqq C_{\infty}\times C_{m}
, with
m\geq 2
. For every even integer
n=2k\geq 0
, the map

\psi\colon L_{n+1}^{h}(\mathbb{Z}[G_{m}])\to\widehat{H}^{n+1}(C_{2};\Wh(G_{m}))

is surjective.

Proof.

We verify that the forgetful map
F\colon L^{s}_{2k}(\mathbb{Z}G_{m})\to L^{h}_{2k}(\mathbb{Z}G_{m})
is injective. The conclusion then follows from the exact sequence (
3.1
).
First we apply Shaneson splitting to the domain and the codomain, to obtain a commutative diagram whose rows are split short exact sequences and whose vertical maps are the forgetful maps
[
52
]
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{s}_{2k}(\mathbb{Z}C_{m})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{s}_{2k}(\mathbb{Z}G_{m})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{h}_{2k-1}(\mathbb{Z}C_{m})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{h}_{2k}(\mathbb{Z}C_{m})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{h}_{2k}(\mathbb{Z}G_{m})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{p}_{2k-1}(\mathbb{Z}C_{m})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}

By the five lemma it suffices to show that the left and right vertical maps are injective.
In
[
2
]
, Bak gave computations of
L_{i}^{X}(\mathbb{Z}G)
for
X\in\{s,h,p\}
and
G
a finite group whose 2-hyperelementary subgroups are abelian, which certainly holds for finite cyclic groups.
See also
[
1
,
3
]

First we note that, by
[
2
, Theorem 8]
, the forgetful map
L^{h}_{2k-1}(\mathbb{Z}C_{m})\to L^{p}_{2k-1}(\mathbb{Z}C_{m})
is injective. So the right vertical map is injective.

To prove that the left vertical map is injective we also use
[
2
]
.
For a finite group
G
whose 2-Sylow subgroup
G_{2}
is normal and abelian, Bak defined
r_{2}:=\rk H^{1}(C_{2};\Wh(G))
. On
[
2
, p. 386]
, he noted that if
G_{2}
is cyclic, as in our case
G=C_{m}
, then
r_{2}=0
. Therefore by the sequence (
3.1
), as shown on
[
2
, p. 390]
, it follows that
L_{2k}^{s}(\mathbb{Z}C_{m})\to L_{2k}^{h}(\mathbb{Z}C_{m})
is injective.
Hence
F\colon L_{2k}^{s}(\mathbb{Z}G_{m})\to L_{2k}^{h}(\mathbb{Z}G_{m})
is injective as desired.
∎

4.
Simple homotopy manifold sets

In this section we prove
E
about the characterisation of simple homotopy manifold sets. First we look at the analogous problem in the setting of CW complexes, which has a simpler answer. Then we consider the case of manifolds, which will rely on the results of
Section

3
.

4.1.
CW complexes

Fix a CW complex
X
and let
G=\pi_{1}(X)
. Our goal is to understand the set
\mathcal{C}^{h}_{s}(X)
defined below.

Definition 4.1
.

Let

\mathcal{C}^{h}_{s}(X)\coloneqq\left\{\text{CW complexes }Y\mid Y\simeq X\right\}/\simeq_{s}.

We will need some auxiliary definitions.

Definition 4.2
.

If
Y
is a CW complex homotopy equivalent to
X
, then let

t_{X}(Y)=\left\{\tau(f)\mid f\colon Y\to X\text{ is a homotopy equivalence}\right\}\subseteq\Wh(G).

Definition 4.3
.

For
x\in\Wh(G)
and
g\in\hAut(X)
let
x^{g}=g_{*}(x)+\tau(g)
. Clearly
x^{\id}=x
and
x^{g\circ g^{\prime}}=(x^{g^{\prime}})^{g}
(see
Proposition

2.31
), so this defines an action of
\hAut(X)
on the set
\Wh(G)
.

With this notation we have the following theorem.

Theorem 4.4
.

The map
t_{X}
induces a well-defined bijection

\widetilde{t}_{X}\colon\mathcal{C}^{h}_{s}(X)\to\Wh(G)/\hAut(X).

The proof will consist of the following sequence of lemmas.

Lemma 4.5
.

If
Y
is a CW complex homotopy equivalent to
X
, then
t_{X}(Y)
is an orbit of the action of
\hAut(X)
on
\Wh(G)
.

Proof.

By
Proposition

2.31
we have
\tau(g\circ f)=\tau(f)^{g}
for every homotopy equivalence
f\colon Y\to X
and
g\in\hAut(X)
. If
f\colon Y\to X
is a homotopy equivalence and
g\in\hAut(X)
, then
g\circ f
is also a homotopy equivalence, so if
x\in t_{X}(Y)
, then
x^{g}\in t_{X}(Y)
for every
g
. On the other hand, if
f,f^{\prime}\colon Y\to X
are homotopy equivalences, then there is a
g\in\hAut(X)
such that
f^{\prime}\simeq g\circ f
, showing that if
x,x^{\prime}\in t_{X}(Y)
, then
x^{\prime}=x^{g}
for some
g
.
∎

Lemma 4.6
.

If
Y
is a CW complex homotopy equivalent to
X
and
Y\simeq_{s}Z
, then
t_{X}(Y)=t_{X}(Z)
.

Proof.

Let
h\colon Z\to Y
be a simple homotopy equivalence. If
x\in t_{X}(Y)
, i.e.
x=\tau(f)
for some homotopy equivalence
f\colon Y\to X
, then
f\circ h\colon Z\to X
is a homotopy equivalence with
\tau(f\circ h)=\tau(f)=x
by
Proposition

2.31
. This shows that
t_{X}(Y)\subseteq t_{X}(Z)
. We get similarly that
t_{X}(Z)\subseteq t_{X}(Y)
, therefore
t_{X}(Y)=t_{X}(Z)
.
∎

Lemma 4.7
.

If
Y
and
Z
are CW complexes homotopy equivalent to
X
and
t_{X}(Y)=t_{X}(Z)
, then
Y\simeq_{s}Z
.

Proof.

Let
x\in t_{X}(Y)=t_{X}(Z)
be an arbitrary element, then there are homotopy equivalences
f\colon Y\to X
and
f^{\prime}\colon Z\to X
with
\tau(f)=\tau(f^{\prime})=x
. Let
f^{-1}\colon X\to Y
denote the homotopy inverse of
f
, then by
Proposition

2.31

0=\tau(\id_{Y})=\tau(f^{-1})+f^{-1}_{*}(\tau(f))=\tau(f^{-1})+f^{-1}_{*}(x)
. Hence we have
\tau(f^{-1}\circ f^{\prime})=\tau(f^{-1})+f^{-1}_{*}(\tau(f^{\prime}))=\tau(f^{-1})+f^{-1}_{*}(x)=0
, showing that
f^{-1}\circ f^{\prime}\colon Z\to Y
is a simple homotopy equivalence.
∎

Lemma 4.8

(
[
15
, (24.1)]
)
.

For every
x\in\Wh(G)
there is a CW complex
Y
and a homotopy equivalence
f\colon Y\rightarrow X
such that
\tau(f)=x
.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

4.4
.

By
Lemma

4.5
,
t_{X}
takes values in
\Wh(G)/\hAut(X)
and by
Lemma

4.6
it induces a well-defined map
\widetilde{t}_{X}
on
\mathcal{C}^{h}_{s}(X)
.
Lemmas

4.7
and
4.8
imply that
\widetilde{t}_{X}
is injective and surjective, respectively.
∎

Remark 4.9
.

There are two special cases when the action of
\hAut(X)
on
\Wh(G)
has a simpler description.

First, assume that
\pi_{1}(g)=\id_{G}
for every
g\in\hAut(X)
. Then
\tau(g\circ g^{\prime})=\tau(g)+\tau(g^{\prime})
for every
g,g^{\prime}\in\hAut(X)
and
x^{g}=x+\tau(g)
for every
x\in\Wh(G)
and
g\in\hAut(X)
. This implies that
\left\{\tau(g)\mid g\in\hAut(X)\right\}
is a subgroup of
\Wh(G)
and
\Wh(G)/\hAut(X)
is the corresponding quotient group.

Second, assume that
\tau(g)=0
for every
g\in\hAut(X)
. Then
x^{g}=g_{*}(x)
for every
x\in\Wh(G)
and
g\in\hAut(X)
. This means that the action of
\hAut(X)
factors through the map
\pi_{1}\colon\hAut(X)\to\Aut(G)
, in particular
\hAut(X)
acts via automorphisms of the group
\Wh(G)
.

4.2.
Manifolds

Now we consider the problem in the manifold setting. Fix a closed connected
\CAT

n
-manifold
M
and let
G=\pi_{1}(M)
with orientation character
w\colon G\rightarrow\left\{\pm 1\right\}
. Then we can consider either all
n
-manifolds that are homotopy equivalent to
M
, or those that are
h
-cobordant to
M
, up to simple homotopy equivalence, or manifolds homotopy equivalent to
M
up to the equivalence relation generated by simple homotopy equivalence and
h
-cobordism.

Definition 4.10
.

Let

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{h}_{s}(M)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{\text{closed $\CAT$ $n$-manifolds }N\mid N\simeq M\right\}/\simeq_{s}

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{\hCob}_{s}(M)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{\text{closed $\CAT$ $n$-manifolds }N\mid N\text{ is $h$-cobordant to }M\right\}/\simeq_{s}

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{h}_{s,\hCob}(M)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{\text{closed $\CAT$ $n$-manifolds }N\mid N\simeq M\right\}/\langle\simeq_{s},\hCob\rangle

where
\langle\simeq_{s},\hCob\rangle
denotes the equivalence relation generated by simple homotopy equivalence and
h
-cobordism.

As before,
\hAut(M)
acts on the set
\Wh(G,w)
, and a subset
t_{M}(N)\subseteq\Wh(G,w)
is defined for every manifold
N
that is homotopy equivalent to
M
. By
Proposition

2.35
if
f\colon N\rightarrow M
is a homotopy equivalence between manifolds, then
\tau(f)\in\mathcal{J}_{n}(G,w)
, so we can also define

u_{M}(N)=\pi(t_{M}(N))=\left\{\pi(\tau(f))\mid f\colon N\to M\text{ is a homotopy equivalence}\right\}

which is a subset of
\widehat{H}^{n+1}(C_{2};\Wh(G,w))
.

Theorem 4.11
.

(a)

The subset
\mathcal{J}_{n}(G,w)\subseteq\Wh(G,w)
is invariant under the action of
\hAut(M)
. The action of
\hAut(M)
on
\mathcal{J}_{n}(G,w)
induces an action on the set
\widehat{H}^{n+1}(C_{2};\Wh(G,w))
.

(b)

The map
t_{M}
induces well-defined injective maps

\widetilde{t}_{M}\colon\mathcal{M}^{h}_{s}(M)\to\mathcal{J}_{n}(G,w)/\hAut(M)\text{ and }\widetilde{t}^{\prime}_{M}\colon\mathcal{M}^{\hCob}_{s}(M)\to q(\mathcal{I}_{n}(G,w)),

where
q\colon\mathcal{J}_{n}(G,w)\to\mathcal{J}_{n}(G,w)/\hAut(M)
denotes the quotient map, so that

q(\mathcal{I}_{n}(G,w))\subseteq\mathcal{J}_{n}(G,w)/\hAut(M).

Similarly, the map
u_{M}
induces a well-defined map

\widetilde{u}_{M}\colon\mathcal{M}^{h}_{s,\hCob}(M)\to\widehat{H}^{n+1}(C_{2};\Wh(G,w))/\hAut(M).

(c)

There is a commutative diagram

\textstyle{\mathcal{M}^{\hCob}_{s}(M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{t}^{\prime}_{M}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{M}^{h}_{s}(M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{t}_{M}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{M}^{h}_{s,\hCob}(M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{u}_{M}}
\textstyle{q(\mathcal{I}_{n}(G,w))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{J}_{n}(G,w)/\hAut(M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widehat{H}^{n+1}(C_{2};\Wh(G,w))/\hAut(M)}

In each row the first map is injective and the second map is surjective. In the top row the composition of the maps is trivial, while the bottom row is an exact sequence of pointed sets.

(d)

If
1.1
are satisfied, then
\widetilde{t}^{\prime}_{M}
is surjective,
\widetilde{u}_{M}
is injective, and the top row is an exact sequence of pointed sets.

(e)

If
1.1
are satisfied, then, using the notation from
Section

3.2
, the subsets

\varrho^{-1}(\image\sigma_{s})\subseteq\mathcal{J}_{n}(G,w)\text{ and }(\varrho\circ\pi)^{-1}(\image\sigma_{s})\subseteq\widehat{H}^{n+1}(C_{2};\Wh(G,w))

are invariant under the action of
\hAut(M)
, and we have

\im\widetilde{t}_{M}=(\varrho\circ\pi)^{-1}(\image\sigma_{s})/\hAut(M)\text{ and }\im\widetilde{u}_{M}=\varrho^{-1}(\image\sigma_{s})/\hAut(M).

This implies
E
(see also
Theorem

4.16
below). Note that parts (
a
), (
b
) and (
c
) do not require
1.1
and so apply to smooth/PL 4-manifolds as well as topological 4-manifolds with arbitrary fundamental group.

We will use the following lemmas in the proof of
Theorem

4.11
.

Lemma 4.12
.

Let
N
and
P
be manifolds homotopy equivalent to
M
. If
N\simeq_{s}P
or
N
is
h
-cobordant to
P
, then
u_{M}(N)=u_{M}(P)
.

Proof.

If
N\simeq_{s}P
, then
t_{M}(N)=t_{M}(P)
by
Lemma

4.6
, so
u_{M}(N)=\pi(t_{M}(N))=\pi(t_{M}(P))=u_{M}(P)
.

If
N
is
h
-cobordant to
P
and
h\colon P\rightarrow N
is a homotopy equivalence induced by an
h
-cobordism, then
\pi(\tau(f\circ h))=\pi(\tau(f))+\pi(f_{*}(\tau(h)))=\pi(\tau(f))
for every homotopy equivalence
f\colon N\rightarrow M
(because
f_{*}(\tau(h))\in\mathcal{I}_{n}(G,w)
by
Proposition

2.38
). Hence
u_{M}(N)\subseteq u_{M}(P)
. We get similarly that
u_{M}(P)\subseteq u_{M}(N)
, therefore
u_{M}(N)=u_{M}(P)
.
∎

Lemma 4.13
.

Suppose that
1.1
are satisfied, and
N
and
P
are manifolds homotopy equivalent to
M
. If
u_{M}(N)=u_{M}(P)
, then there is a manifold
Q
that is simple homotopy equivalent to
N
and
h
-cobordant to
P
.

Proof.

Since
u_{M}(N)=u_{M}(P)
, there are homotopy equivalences
f\colon N\rightarrow M
and
g\colon P\rightarrow M
such that
\pi(\tau(f))=\pi(\tau(g))
, equivalently,
\tau(f)-\tau(g)\in\mathcal{I}_{n}(G,w)
. If
g^{-1}
denotes the homotopy inverse of
g
, then
\tau(g^{-1}\circ f)=\tau(g^{-1})+g^{-1}_{*}(\tau(f))=\tau(g^{-1})+g^{-1}_{*}(\tau(g))+g^{-1}_{*}(\tau(f)-\tau(g))=\tau(g^{-1}\circ g)+g^{-1}_{*}(\tau(f)-\tau(g))=g^{-1}_{*}(\tau(f)-\tau(g))
by
Proposition

2.31
. So we can apply
Corollary

3.3
to the homotopy equivalence
g^{-1}\circ f\colon N\rightarrow P
.
∎

Lemma 4.14
.

Suppose that
N
and
P
are manifolds satisfying
1.1
. If there is a manifold
Q
that is simple homotopy equivalent to
N
and
h
-cobordant to
P
, then there is a manifold
R
that is
h
-cobordant to
N
and simple homotopy equivalent to
P
.

Proof.

Apply
Corollary

3.3
to the composition of a homotopy equivalence
P\rightarrow Q
induced by an
h
-cobordism and a simple homotopy
Q\rightarrow N
.
∎

Proposition 4.15
.

Suppose that
N
and
P
are manifolds satisfying
1.1
. Then the following are equivalent.

(a)

The manifolds
N
and
P
are equivalent under the equivalence relation generated by simple homotopy equivalence and
h
-cobordism.

(a)

There is a manifold
Q
that is simple homotopy equivalent to
N
and
h
-cobordant to
P
.

Proof.

A chain between
N
and
P
of alternating simple homotopy equivalences and
h
-cobordisms can be reduced to a chain of length two using
Lemma

4.14
.
∎

Now we are ready to begin the proof of
Theorem

4.11
.

Proof of
Theorem

4.11
.

(
a
) If
g\in\hAut(M)
, then
\tau(g)\in\mathcal{J}_{n}(G,w)
by
Proposition

2.35
. Moreover,
\pi_{1}(g)\circ w=w
, hence
g_{*}\colon\Wh(G,w)\rightarrow\Wh(G,w)
is compatible with the involution, so if
x=-(-1)^{n}\overline{x}
, then
g_{*}(x)=-(-1)^{n}\overline{g_{*}(x)}
. Hence, if
x\in\mathcal{J}_{n}(G,w)
, then
x^{g}\in\mathcal{J}_{n}(G,w)
, i.e.
\hAut(M)
acts on
\mathcal{J}_{n}(G,w)
.

If
x,y\in\mathcal{J}_{n}(G,w)
and
x-y\in\mathcal{I}_{n}(G,w)
, then
x^{g}-y^{g}=(g_{*}(x)+\tau(g))-(g_{*}(y)+\tau(g))=g_{*}(x-y)\in\mathcal{I}_{n}(G,w)
(because
g_{*}
is compatible with the involution). Therefore there is an induced action on
\widehat{H}^{n+1}(C_{2};\Wh(G,w))
.

(
b
) If
f\colon N\rightarrow M
is a homotopy equivalence for some
n
-manifold
N
, then
\tau(f)\in\mathcal{J}_{n}(G,w)
by
Proposition

2.35
. Hence
t_{M}(N)\subseteq\mathcal{J}_{n}(G,w)
for every manifold
N
that is homotopy equivalent to
M
.
Lemmas

4.5
,
4.6
, and
4.7
can be used again to show that
t_{M}
induces well-defined injective maps
\widetilde{t}_{M}\colon\mathcal{M}^{h}_{s}(M)\to\mathcal{J}_{n}(G,w)/\hAut(M)
and
\widetilde{t}^{\prime}_{M}\colon\mathcal{M}^{\hCob}_{s}(M)\to\mathcal{J}_{n}(G,w)/\hAut(M)
. If
N
is
h
-cobordant to
M
and
f\colon N\rightarrow M
is a homotopy equivalence induced by an
h
-cobordism, then
\tau(f)\in\mathcal{I}_{n}(G,w)
by
Proposition

2.38
, showing that
t_{M}(N)\in q(\mathcal{I}_{n}(G,w))
.

The analogue of
Lemma

4.5
shows that
u_{M}(N)
is an orbit of the action of
\hAut(M)
on
\widehat{H}^{n+1}(C_{2};\Wh(G,w))
(using that
\pi(\tau(g\circ f))=\pi(\tau(f)^{g})=\pi(\tau(f))^{g}
for every homotopy equivalence
f\colon N\to M
and
g\in\hAut(M)
).
Lemma

4.12
shows that
u_{M}
induces a well-defined map on
\mathcal{M}^{h}_{s,\hCob}(M)
.

(
c
) This follows immediately from the definitions.

(
d
) The surjectivity of
\widetilde{t}^{\prime}_{M}
and injectivity of
\widetilde{u}_{M}
follow from
Corollary

3.2
and
Lemma

4.13
, respectively.

For the exactness of the top row, assume that
N
is equivalent to
M
under the equivalence relation generated by simple homotopy equivalence and
h
-cobordism. By
Proposition

4.15
there is a manifold
P
that is simple homotopy equivalent to
N
and
h
-cobordant to
M
. This represents an element of
\mathcal{M}^{\hCob}_{s}(M)
, which is mapped to the equivalence class of
N
in
\mathcal{M}^{h}_{s}(M)
.

(
e
) First consider
\varrho^{-1}(\image\sigma_{s})
, which is equal to
\image\widehat{\tau}
by
Corollary

3.9
. This is
\hAut(M)
-invariant, because for any
[N,f]\in\mathcal{S}^{h}(M)
and
g\in\hAut(M)
we have
\pi(\tau(f))^{g}=\pi(\tau(g\circ f))=\widehat{\tau}([N,g\circ f])
. Since the action of
\hAut(M)
on
\widehat{H}^{n+1}(C_{2};\Wh(G,w))
is induced by its action on
\mathcal{J}_{n}(G,w)
, and
\varrho^{-1}(\image\sigma_{s})
is
\hAut(M)
-invariant,
\pi^{-1}(\varrho^{-1}(\image\sigma_{s}))
is
\hAut(M)
-invariant too.

Now suppose that
[N]\in\mathcal{M}^{h}_{s,\hCob}(M)
. Then there is a homotopy equivalence
f\colon N\rightarrow M
and
\widetilde{u}_{M}([N])
is the orbit of
\pi(\tau(f))=\widehat{\tau}([N,f])\in\image\widehat{\tau}=\varrho^{-1}(\image\sigma_{s})
. This shows that
\im\widetilde{u}_{M}\subseteq\varrho^{-1}(\image\sigma_{s})/\hAut(M)
. On the other hand, if
x\in\varrho^{-1}(\image\sigma_{s})=\image\widehat{\tau}
, then
x=\pi(\tau(f))
for some homotopy equivalence
f\colon N\rightarrow M
, and then
[N]\in\mathcal{M}^{h}_{s,\hCob}(M)
and
\widetilde{u}_{M}([N])
is the orbit of
x
. Therefore
\im\widetilde{u}_{M}\supseteq\varrho^{-1}(\image\sigma_{s})/\hAut(M)
.

By the commutativity of the diagram in part (
c
),
\im\widetilde{t}_{M}
is contained in the inverse image of
\im\widetilde{u}_{M}=\varrho^{-1}(\image\sigma_{s})/\hAut(M)
, which is
\pi^{-1}(\varrho^{-1}(\image\sigma_{s}))/\hAut(M)
. In the other direction, if
x\in\mathcal{J}_{n}(G,w)
and
\pi(x)\in\varrho^{-1}(\image\sigma_{s})=\image\widehat{\tau}
, then there exists a manifold
N_{1}
and a homotopy equivalence
f_{1}\colon N_{1}\to M
with
\pi(\tau(f_{1}))=\pi(x)
. It follows that
x=\tau(f_{1})+y
for some
y\in\mathcal{I}_{n}(G,w)
. Use
(f_{1})_{*}
to identify
\pi_{1}(N_{1})
with
G=\pi_{1}(M)
(since
f_{1}
is a homotopy equivalence, the orientation character of
N
is also
w
). By
Corollary

3.2
there exists a manifold
N_{2}
and a homotopy equivalence
f_{2}\colon N_{2}\to N_{1}
with
\tau(f_{2})=y
. By
Proposition

2.31
we have
\tau(f_{1}\circ f_{2})=\tau(f_{1})+(f_{1})_{*}(\tau(f_{2}))=\tau(f_{1})+\tau(f_{2})=\tau(f_{1})+y=x
(where
(f_{1})_{*}=\Id
because we used
f_{1}
to identify
\pi_{1}(N_{1})
with
G
). So for
[N_{2}]\in\mathcal{M}^{h}_{s}(M)
we get that
\widetilde{t}_{M}([N_{2}])
is the orbit of
x
, showing that
\im\widetilde{t}_{M}\supseteq(\varrho\circ\pi)^{-1}(\image\sigma_{s})/\hAut(M)
.
∎

In particular we have the following, which is immediate from
Theorem

4.11
.

Theorem 4.16
.

If
1.1
are satisfied, then there are bijections

\displaystyle\widetilde{t}^{\prime}_{M}

\displaystyle\colon\mathcal{M}^{\hCob}_{s}(M)\xrightarrow{\cong}q(\mathcal{I}_{n}(G,w))

\displaystyle\widetilde{t}_{M}

\displaystyle\colon\mathcal{M}^{h}_{s}(M)\xrightarrow{\cong}(\varrho\circ\pi)^{-1}(\image\sigma_{s})/\hAut(M)

\displaystyle\widetilde{u}_{M}

\displaystyle\colon\mathcal{M}^{h}_{s,\hCob}(M)\xrightarrow{\cong}\varrho^{-1}(\image\sigma_{s})/\hAut(M).

With extra input from the map
\psi
, the latter two statements become cleaner.

Corollary 4.17
.

If
1.1
are satisfied and
\psi
is surjective, then there are bijections
\mathcal{M}^{h}_{s}(M)\cong\mathcal{J}_{n}(G,w)/\hAut(M)
and
\mathcal{M}^{h}_{s,\hCob}(M)\cong\widehat{H}^{n+1}(C_{2};\Wh(G,w))/\hAut(M)
.

Proof.

By
Corollary

3.9
(
b
),
\widehat{H}^{n+1}(C_{2};\Wh(G,w))=\image\psi\subseteq\varrho^{-1}(\image\sigma_{s})
. Hence
\varrho^{-1}(\image\sigma_{s})=\widehat{H}^{n+1}(C_{2};\Wh(G,w))
,
(\varrho\circ\pi)^{-1}(\image\sigma_{s})=\mathcal{J}_{n}(G,w)
.
∎

Remark 4.18
.

If
\tau(g)\in\mathcal{I}_{n}(G,w)
for every
g\in\hAut(M)
, then the proof of
Theorem

4.11
(
a
) shows that
\mathcal{I}_{n}(G,w)
is also an invariant subset under the action of
\hAut(M)
, and
q(\mathcal{I}_{n}(G,w))=\mathcal{I}_{n}(G,w)/\hAut(M)
. It also means that
\pi(\tau(g))=0
for every
g
, so
\hAut(M)
acts on the group
\widehat{H}^{n+1}(C_{2};\Wh(G,w))
via automorphisms.

As in
Remark

4.9
, if
\tau(g)=0
for every
g
, then
\hAut(M)
acts on the groups
\mathcal{I}_{n}(G,w)
and
\mathcal{J}_{n}(G,w)
via automorphisms. And if
\pi_{1}(g)=\id_{G}
for every
g
, then
\mathcal{J}_{n}(G,w)/\hAut(M)
and
\widehat{H}^{n+1}(C_{2};\Wh(G,w))/\hAut(M)
are quotient groups of
\mathcal{J}_{n}(G,w)
and
\widehat{H}^{n+1}(C_{2};\Wh(G,w))
.

Now we consider some corollaries of
Theorems

4.11
and
4.16
.

Definition 4.19
.

Let
M
be a closed,
\CAT

n
-manifold with
\pi_{1}(M)\cong G
and orientation character
w\colon G\rightarrow\left\{\pm 1\right\}
. We define the subsets

\displaystyle T(M)

\displaystyle=\left\{\tau(g)\mid g\in\hAut(M)\right\}\subseteq\mathcal{J}_{n}(G,w)

\displaystyle U(M)

\displaystyle=\left\{\pi(\tau(g))\mid g\in\hAut(M)\right\}\subseteq\widehat{H}^{n+1}(C_{2};\Wh(G,w))

Proposition 4.20
.

Let
M
be a closed,
\CAT

n
-manifold satisfying
1.1
. Let
G=\pi_{1}(M)
with orientation character
w\colon G\rightarrow\left\{\pm 1\right\}
. Then
|\mathcal{M}^{\hCob}_{s}(M)|>1

if and only if
\mathcal{I}_{n}(G,w)\setminus T(M)
is nonempty.

Proof.

Note that
T(M)=t_{M}(M)\subseteq\mathcal{J}_{n}(G,w)
is the orbit of
0
under the action of
\hAut(M)
. So
q(\mathcal{I}_{n}(G,w))
contains more than one element if and only if
\mathcal{I}_{n}(G,w)\setminus T(M)
is nonempty. By
Theorem

4.16
this is equivalent to
\mathcal{M}^{\hCob}_{s}(M)
containing more than one element.
∎

Proposition 4.21
.

Let
M
be a closed,
\CAT

n
-manifold satisfying
1.1
. Let
G=\pi_{1}(M)
with orientation character
w\colon G\rightarrow\left\{\pm 1\right\}
. Then
|\mathcal{M}^{h}_{s,\hCob}(M)|>1

if and only if
\varrho^{-1}(\image\sigma_{s})\setminus U(M)
is nonempty. In particular, it is a sufficient condition that
\image(\psi)\setminus U(M)
is nonempty.

Proof.

Again
U(M)=u_{M}(M)\subseteq\widehat{H}^{n+1}(C_{2};\Wh(G,w))
is the orbit of
0
under the action of
\hAut(M)
. So
\varrho^{-1}(\image\sigma_{s})/\hAut(M)
contains more than one element if and only if
\varrho^{-1}(\image\sigma_{s})\setminus U(M)
is nonempty. By
Theorem

4.16
this is equivalent to
\mathcal{M}^{h}_{s,\hCob}(M)
containing more than one element. Finally, by
Corollary

3.9
(
b
) we have
\varrho^{-1}(\image\sigma_{s})\setminus U(M)\supseteq\image(\psi)\setminus U(M)
.
∎

Proposition 4.22
.

Let
M
be a closed,
\CAT

n
-manifold satisfying
1.1
. Then the following are equivalent:

(i)

|\mathcal{M}^{h}_{s}(M)|>1
.

(ii)

Either
|\mathcal{M}^{\hCob}_{s}(M)|>1
or
|\mathcal{M}^{h}_{s,\hCob}(M)|>1
.

(iii)

(\varrho\circ\pi)^{-1}(\image\sigma_{s})\setminus T(M)
is nonempty.

Proof.

\eqref{item:prop-he-not-she-equiv-i}\Leftrightarrow\eqref{item:prop-he-not-she-equiv-ii}
. By
Theorem

4.11
(
c
) and (
d
) we have
|\mathcal{M}^{h}_{s}(M)|=1
if and only if
|\mathcal{M}^{\hCob}_{s}(M)|=1
and
|\mathcal{M}^{h}_{s,\hCob}(M)|=1
.

\eqref{item:prop-he-not-she-equiv-i}\Leftrightarrow\eqref{item:prop-he-not-she-equiv-iii}
. Follows from
Theorem

4.16
since
T(M)
is the orbit of
0
.
∎

Part II
The simple homotopy manifold set of
S^{1}\times L

In this part we consider
S^{1}\times L
, the product of the circle with a lens space
L
. We will prove
B
,
C
, and
D
about
the simple homotopy manifold sets of
S^{1}\times L
.
As described in
Sections

1.4
and
1.5
, we need to study the involution on
\Wh(C_{\infty}\times C_{m})
and the group
\hAut(S^{1}\times L)
.
Section

5
contains our results about the groups
\mathcal{J}_{n}(C_{\infty}\times C_{m})
,
\mathcal{I}_{n}(C_{\infty}\times C_{m})
and
\widehat{H}^{n+1}(C_{2};\Wh(C_{\infty}\times C_{m}))
(for
n
even), which rely on the results of
Part

III
. In
Section

6
, we prove that all homotopy automorphisms of
S^{1}\times L
are simple, and determine the automorphisms of
C_{\infty}\times C_{m}
induced by them. In
Section

7
, we combine these results to prove
B
,
C
, and
D
.

5.
The involution on
\Wh(C_{\infty}\times C_{m})

In order to understand its involution, we first consider a direct sum decomposition of the Whitehead group
\Wh(C_{\infty}\times G)
. This decomposition is derived from the fundamental theorem for
K_{1}(\mathbb{Z}G[t,t^{-1}])

[
7
]
; see also
[
52
]
,
[
76
, III.3.6]
.
Whilst this theorem appeared for the first time in Bass’ book, the paper of Bass-Heller-Swan
[
5
, Theorem 2’]
is often mentioned in conjunction with it, as an early version which contained several key ideas, and the theorem for left regular rings, appeared there. See
[
7
, pXV]
for further discussion.

We will use the version given by Ranicki
[
52
]
. We start by defining the terms appearing in the decomposition in
Section

5.1
.
Section

5.2
contains the decomposition of
\Wh(C_{\infty}\times C_{m})
and the induced decompositions of
\mathcal{J}_{n}(C_{\infty}\times C_{m})
,
\mathcal{I}_{n}(C_{\infty}\times C_{m})
and
\widehat{H}^{n+1}(C_{2};\Wh(C_{\infty}\times C_{m}))
. These will be combined with the results of
Part

III
in
Section

5.3
, to prove
Theorems

5.14
,
5.15
, and
5.17
, which will be the key algebraic ingredients in the proofs of
B
,
C
, and
D
respectively.

5.1.
The
\widetilde{K}_{0}
and
NK_{1}
groups

First we consider the
K_{0}
groups. For a ring
R
, we will define the abelian group
K_{0}(R)
, and, for a group
G
, the reduced group
\widetilde{K}_{0}(\mathbb{Z}G)
. A convenient reference for this material is
[
76
]
. In
Part

III
, we will study the involution on
\widetilde{K}_{0}(\mathbb{Z}G)
in more detail when
G
is a finite cyclic group.

Definition 5.1
.

For a ring
R
let
P(R)
denote the set of isomorphism classes of (finitely generated left) projective
R
-modules, which is a monoid under direct sum. Define
K_{0}(R)
to be the Grothendieck group of this monoid,
i.e. the abelian group generated by symbols
[P]
, for every
P\in P(R)
, subject to the relations
[P_{1}\oplus P_{2}]=[P_{1}]+[P_{2}]
for
P_{1},P_{2}\in P(R)
.

The assignment
R\mapsto K_{0}(R)
defines a functor from the category of rings to the category of abelian groups. If
f\colon R\to S
is a ring homomorphism, then
K_{0}(f)\colon K_{0}(R)\to K_{0}(S)
is the map induced by extension of scalars
P\mapsto f_{\#}(P):=S\otimes_{R}P
(see
Definition

2.21
) for
P\in P(R)
.

Definition 5.2
.

For a group
G
, let

\widetilde{K}_{0}(\mathbb{Z}G)\coloneqq K_{0}(\mathbb{Z}G)/K_{0}(\mathbb{Z}),

where the map
K_{0}(\mathbb{Z})\to K_{0}(\mathbb{Z}G)
is induced by the inclusion
i\colon\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}G
.

Definition 5.3
.

For a group
G
and a (finitely generated) projective
\mathbb{Z}G
-module
P
, the
rank
of
P
is defined to be

\rk(P):=\rank_{\mathbb{Z}}(\varepsilon_{\#}(P))

where
\varepsilon\colon\mathbb{Z}G\to\mathbb{Z}
denotes the augmentation map and
\rank_{\mathbb{Z}}
denotes the rank of a free
\mathbb{Z}
-module.

We have that
K_{0}(\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}
and, for
P\in P(\mathbb{Z}G)
, the rank
\rk(P)
coincides the the image of
[P]
under the composition
K_{0}(\mathbb{Z}G)\to K_{0}(\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}
induced by
\varepsilon
. Since
\varepsilon\circ i=\id_{\mathbb{Z}}
, we have a splitting of abelian groups

K_{0}(\mathbb{Z}G)\cong\mathbb{Z}\oplus\widetilde{K}_{0}(\mathbb{Z}G)

given by
[P]\mapsto(\rank(P),[P])
.
In particular,
\widetilde{K}_{0}(\mathbb{Z}G)
is the set of equivalence classes of projective
\mathbb{Z}G
-modules
P
where
P\sim Q
if
P\oplus\mathbb{Z}G^{i}\cong Q\oplus\mathbb{Z}G^{j}
for some
i,j\geq 0
.

For
R
a ring with involution, we can define a natural involution on
K_{0}(R)
. If
P\in P(R)
, then
P^{*}\in P(R)
(see
Definition

2.23
) since
P\oplus Q\cong R^{n}
implies that
P^{*}\oplus Q^{*}\cong R^{n}
.

Definition 5.4
.

The
standard involution
on
K_{0}(R)
is given by
[P]\mapsto-[P^{*}]
. This map preserves the rank of projective
\mathbb{Z}G
-modules and so induces an involution on
\widetilde{K}_{0}(\mathbb{Z}G)
.

Next we define the Nil groups
NK_{1}(R)
and recall some of their properties.

Definition 5.5
.

For a ring
R
, let

NK_{1}(R)\coloneqq\coker(K_{1}(R)\rightarrow K_{1}(R[t])),

where the map
K_{1}(R)\rightarrow K_{1}(R[t])
is induced by the inclusion
R\rightarrow R[t]
.

Note that
NK_{1}
is a functor from the category of rings to the category of abelian groups.
If
R
(and hence
R[t]
) is a ring with involution, then
K_{1}(R[t])
also has a natural involution (see
Section

2.2
). This involution preserves the image of
K_{1}(R)\rightarrow K_{1}(R[t])
, and hence induces an involution on
NK_{1}(R)
, which we will denote by
x\mapsto\overline{x}
.

Definition 5.6
.

We equip
NK_{1}(R)^{2}=NK_{1}(R)\oplus NK_{1}(R)
with the involution
(x,y)\mapsto(\overline{y},\overline{x})
.

We will need the following result of Farrell
[
18
]
(note that
\mathrm{Nil}
, which is also written as
\mathrm{Nil}_{0}
, coincides with
NK_{1}
by
[
7
, Chapter XII; 7.4(a)]
).

Theorem 5.7

(Farrell)
.

For any ring
R
, if
NK_{1}(R)\neq 0
, then
NK_{1}(R)
is not finitely generated.

The following can be proven by combining results of Bass-Murthy
[
6
]
, Martin
[
41
]
, and Weibel
[
77
]
.

Theorem 5.8
.

Let
m\geq 1
. Then
NK_{1}(\mathbb{Z}C_{m})=0
if and only if
m
is square-free.

Proof.

If
m
is square-free, then
NK_{1}(\mathbb{Z}C_{m})=0
by
[
6
, Theorem 10.8 (d)]
. If
n\mid m
and
NK_{1}(\mathbb{Z}C_{n})\neq 0
, then
NK_{1}(\mathbb{Z}C_{m})\neq 0
(by, for example,
[
41
, Theorem 3.6]
). It remains to show that
NK_{1}(\mathbb{Z}C_{p^{2}})\neq 0
for all primes
p
. This was achieved in the case where
p
is odd in
[
41
, Theorem B]
and in the case
p=2
in
[
77
, Theorem 1.4]
. ∎

5.2.
Bass-Heller-Swan decomposition of
\Wh(C_{\infty}\times G)

By
[
52
, p.329, p.357]
we have the following theorem.

Theorem 5.9

(Bass-Heller-Swan decomposition)
.

Let
G
be a group. Then there is an isomorphism of
\mathbb{Z}C_{2}
-modules, which is natural in
G
:

\Wh(C_{\infty}\times G)\cong\Wh(G)\oplus\widetilde{K}_{0}(\mathbb{Z}G)\oplus NK_{1}(\mathbb{Z}G)^{2}

where the
\mathbb{Z}C_{2}
-module structure of each component is determined by the involution defined in
Section

2.2
,
Definition

5.4
and
Definition

5.6
respectively.

Proposition 5.10
.

Let
G
be a group. The decomposition of
Theorem

5.9
restricts to the following isomorphisms:

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{n}(C_{\infty}\times G)

\displaystyle\cong\mathcal{J}_{n}(G)\oplus\{x\in\widetilde{K}_{0}(\mathbb{Z}G)\mid x=-(-1)^{n}\overline{x}\}\oplus NK_{1}(\mathbb{Z}G)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{n}(C_{\infty}\times G)

\displaystyle\cong\mathcal{I}_{n}(G)\oplus\{x-(-1)^{n}\overline{x}\mid x\in\widetilde{K}_{0}(\mathbb{Z}G)\}\oplus NK_{1}(\mathbb{Z}G)

where
NK_{1}(\mathbb{Z}G)
is embedded into
NK_{1}(\mathbb{Z}G)^{2}
by the map
x\mapsto(x,-(-1)^{n}\overline{x})
.

Proof.

Since
Theorem

5.9
gives a decomposition of
\mathbb{Z}C_{2}
-modules,
\mathcal{I}_{n}(C_{\infty}\times G)
and
\mathcal{J}_{n}(C_{\infty}\times G)
are decomposed into the corresponding subgroups of the components on the right-hand side. If
(x,y)\in NK_{1}(\mathbb{Z}G)^{2}
, then
\overline{(x,y)}=(\overline{y},\overline{x})
, so
(x,y)=-(-1)^{n}\overline{(x,y)}
if and only if
y=-(-1)^{n}\overline{x}
, so

\{(x,y)\in NK_{1}(\mathbb{Z}G)^{2}\mid(x,y)=-(-1)^{n}\overline{(x,y)}\}=\{(x,-(-1)^{n}\overline{x})\mid x\in NK_{1}(\mathbb{Z}G)\}

which is isomorphic to
NK_{1}(\mathbb{Z}G)
.
Similarly, we have

(x,y)-(-1)^{n}\overline{(x,y)}=(x-(-1)^{n}\overline{y},-(-1)^{n}\overline{x-(-1)^{n}\overline{y}}).

Therefore we have that

\{(x,y)-(-1)^{n}\overline{(x,y)}\mid(x,y)\in NK_{1}(\mathbb{Z}G)^{2}\}=\{(x,-(-1)^{n}\overline{x})\mid x\in NK_{1}(\mathbb{Z}G)\}

which is isomorphic to
NK_{1}(\mathbb{Z}G)
.
∎

We immediately get the following corollary; see also
[
52
, p.358]
.

Corollary 5.11
.

Let
G
be a group. The decomposition of
Theorem

5.9
induces an isomorphism

\widehat{H}^{n+1}(C_{2};\Wh(C_{\infty}\times G))\cong\widehat{H}^{n+1}(C_{2};\Wh(G))\oplus\widehat{H}^{n+1}(C_{2};\widetilde{K}_{0}(\mathbb{Z}G)).

5.3.
The groups
\mathcal{J}_{n}(C_{\infty}\times C_{m})
,
\mathcal{I}_{n}(C_{\infty}\times C_{m})
, and
\widehat{H}^{n+1}(C_{2};\Wh(C_{\infty}\times C_{m}))

In this section we prove, using results from
Part

III
, our main results about the groups
\mathcal{J}_{n}(C_{\infty}\times C_{m})
,
\mathcal{I}_{n}(C_{\infty}\times C_{m})
, and
\widehat{H}^{n+1}(C_{2};\Wh(C_{\infty}\times C_{m}))
for
n
even.

First note that by
Proposition

5.10
and
Theorem

5.7
, for any group
G
and any
n
, if
NK_{1}(\mathbb{Z}G)\neq 0
, then
\mathcal{J}_{n}(C_{\infty}\times G)
and
\mathcal{I}_{n}(C_{\infty}\times G)
are not finitely generated. If
G
is finite and
n
is even, then we also have the following.

Lemma 5.12
.

Suppose that
n
is even and
G
is a finite group. Then the following are equivalent:

(i)

|\mathcal{J}_{n}(C_{\infty}\times G)|<\infty

(ii)

|\mathcal{I}_{n}(C_{\infty}\times G)|<\infty

(iii)

NK_{1}(\mathbb{Z}G)=0
.

Proof.

Let
SK_{1}(\mathbb{Z}G)=\ker(K_{1}(\mathbb{Z}G)\to K_{1}(\mathbb{Q}G))
where the map is induced by inclusion
\mathbb{Z}G\subseteq\mathbb{Q}G
. It was shown by Wall
[
71
, Proposition 6.5]
(see also
[
48
, Theorem 7.4]
) that
SK_{1}(\mathbb{Z}G)
is isomorphic to the torsion subgroup of
\Wh(G)
. Let
\Wh^{\prime}(G)=\Wh(G)/SK_{1}(\mathbb{Z}G)
be the free part. The standard involution on
\Wh(G)
induces an involution on
\Wh^{\prime}(G)
, and Wall showed this induced involution is the identity (see
[
48
, Corollary 7.5]
).

It follows that if
x=-\overline{x}\in\Wh(G)
, then
x
maps to
0\in\Wh^{\prime}(G)
, so
x\in SK_{1}(\mathbb{Z}G)
. Hence
\mathcal{I}_{n}(G)\leq\mathcal{J}_{n}(G)\leq SK_{1}(\mathbb{Z}G)
.
If
G
is finite, then
SK_{1}(\mathbb{Z}G)
is finite
[
71
]
and
\widetilde{K}_{0}(\mathbb{Z}G)
is finite
[
65
]
(see also
Proposition

8.4
(
ii
)).
Therefore the lemma follows from
Proposition

5.10
and
Theorem

5.7
.
∎

Proposition 5.13
.

Suppose that
n
is even. Then for every
m\geq 2
we have
\mathcal{J}_{n}(C_{m})=0
, and hence
\mathcal{I}_{n}(C_{m})\cong\widehat{H}^{n+1}(C_{2};\Wh(C_{m}))=0
.

Proof.

By
Proposition

2.10
,
\Wh(C_{m})
is a finitely generated free abelian group. By
[
8
, Proposition 4.2]
, the involution acts trivially on
\Wh(G)
for all
G
finite abelian. Hence
\mathcal{J}_{n}(C_{m})=\{x\in\Wh(C_{m})\mid x=-x\}=0
.
∎

The next three theorems are the main results of
Section

5
. They will be established as a consequence of Theorems
11.1
,
11.2
and
11.3
, which will be proven in
Section

11
(which is in
Part

III
). These results were stated in the introduction as Theorems
1.6
,
1.7
and
1.8
respectively.

Theorem 5.14
.

Suppose that
n
is even and
m\geq 2
is an integer.

(i)

|\mathcal{J}_{n}(C_{\infty}\times C_{m})|=1
if and only if
m\in\{2,3,5,6,7,10,11,13,14,17,19\}
;

(ii)

|\mathcal{J}_{n}(C_{\infty}\times C_{m})|=\infty
if and only if
m
is not square-free;

(iii)

|\mathcal{J}_{n}(C_{\infty}\times C_{m})|\to\infty
super-exponentially in
m
.

Theorem 5.15
.

Suppose that
n
is even and
m\geq 2
is an integer.

(i)

|\mathcal{I}_{n}(C_{\infty}\times C_{m})|=1
if and only if
m\in\{2,3,5,6,7,10,11,13,14,15,17,19,29\}
;

(ii)

|\mathcal{I}_{n}(C_{\infty}\times C_{m})|=\infty
if and only if
m
is not square-free;

(iii)

|\mathcal{I}_{n}(C_{\infty}\times C_{m})|\to\infty
super-exponentially in
m
.

Remark 5.16
.

To write
Theorem

5.14
(
i
) another way, the
m\geq 2
for which
|\mathcal{J}_{n}(C_{\infty}\times C_{m})|=1
are as follows:

m=\begin{cases}\text{$p$},&\text{where $p\leq 19$ is prime}\\
\text{$2p$},&\text{where $p\leq 7$ is an odd prime}.\end{cases}

Furthermore,
Theorem

5.15
(
i
) states that
|\mathcal{I}_{n}(C_{\infty}\times C_{m})|=1
if and only if
|\mathcal{J}_{n}(C_{\infty}\times C_{m})|=1
or
m\in\{15,29\}
.

Proof of
Theorems

5.14
and
5.15
.

First note that
Theorem

5.14
(
ii
) and
Theorem

5.15
(
ii
) immediately follow from
Theorem

5.8
and
Lemma

5.12
. For the proofs of (
i
) and (
iii
), we can therefore restrict to the case where
m
is square-free, when
NK_{1}(\mathbb{Z}C_{m})=0
. By
Propositions

5.10
and
5.13
we then have:

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{n}(C_{\infty}\times C_{m})

\displaystyle\cong\{x\in\widetilde{K}_{0}(\mathbb{Z}C_{m})\mid\overline{x}=-x\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{n}(C_{\infty}\times C_{m})

\displaystyle\cong\{x-\overline{x}\mid x\in\widetilde{K}_{0}(\mathbb{Z}C_{m})\}.

The results follow from
Theorems

11.1
and
11.2
(see
Section

11
).
∎

Theorem 5.17
.

Suppose that
n
is even and
m\geq 2
is an integer.

(i)

|\widehat{H}^{n+1}(C_{2};\Wh(C_{\infty}\times C_{m}))|=1
for infinitely many
m
.

(ii)

|\widehat{H}^{n+1}(C_{2};\Wh(C_{\infty}\times C_{m}))|<\infty
for every
m
.

(iii)

\displaystyle\sup_{l\leq m}|\widehat{H}^{n+1}(C_{2};\Wh(C_{\infty}\times C_{l}))|\to\infty
exponentially in
m
.

Note that (
i
) and (
iii
) imply that

\liminf_{m\to\infty}|\widehat{H}^{n+1}(C_{2};\Wh(C_{\infty}\times C_{m}))|=1,\limsup_{m\to\infty}|\widehat{H}^{n+1}(C_{2};\Wh(C_{\infty}\times C_{m}))|=\infty.

Proof.

It follows from
Corollary

5.11
and
Proposition

5.13
that

\widehat{H}^{n+1}(C_{2};\Wh(C_{\infty}\times C_{m}))\cong\widehat{H}^{1}(C_{2};\widetilde{K}_{0}(\mathbb{Z}C_{m}))\cong\frac{\{x\in\widetilde{K}_{0}(\mathbb{Z}C_{m})\mid x=-\overline{x}\}}{\{x-\overline{x}\mid x\in\widetilde{K}_{0}(\mathbb{Z}C_{m})\}}.

Hence parts (
i
) and (
iii
) follows from
Theorem

11.3
, which will be proved in
Section

11
. Part (
ii
) follows from the fact that
\widetilde{K}_{0}(\mathbb{Z}C_{m})
is finite
[
65
]
(see also
Proposition

8.4
(
ii
)).
∎

6.
Homotopy automorphisms of
S^{1}\times L

Fix
k,m\geq 2
and
q_{1},\ldots,q_{k}
such that
\gcd(m,q_{j})=1
. Let
L=L_{2k-1}(m;q_{1},\ldots,q_{k})
be a
(2k-1)
-dimensional lens space with
\pi_{1}(L)\cong C_{m}
. We will be interested in the product
S^{1}\times L
and its homotopy automorphisms. Fix basepoints of
S^{1}
and
L
and let
i_{1}\colon S^{1}\rightarrow S^{1}\times L
and
i_{2}\colon L\rightarrow S^{1}\times L
denote the standard embeddings, we will identify
S^{1}
and
L
with their images in
S^{1}\times L
. Let
G=C_{\infty}\times C_{m}\cong\pi_{1}(S^{1}\times L)
.

6.1.
The automorphism induced on the fundamental group

Our first goal is to determine which automorphisms of
G
can be realised by homotopy automorphisms of
S^{1}\times L
, i.e. to describe the image of the map
\pi_{1}\colon\hAut(S^{1}\times L)\rightarrow\Aut(G)
. Note that the automorphisms of
G=C_{\infty}\times C_{m}
can be expressed as matrices of the form
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
0&c\end{smallmatrix}\right)
, where
a\in\{\pm 1\}
,
b\in C_{m}
and
c\in C_{m}^{\times}\cong\Aut(C_{m})
.

Lemma 6.1

(
[
15
, Statement 29.5]
)
.

Let
c\in C_{m}^{\times}
. There is a homotopy automorphism
f\colon L\rightarrow L
such that
\pi_{1}(f)=c
if and only if
c^{k}\equiv\pm 1\,\,\text{mod}\,m
.

Lemma 6.2
.

(a)

There is a diffeomorphism
f\in\Diff(S^{1}\times L)
such that
\pi_{1}(f)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}-1&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.

(b)

There is a diffeomorphism
f\in\Diff(S^{1}\times L)
such that
\pi_{1}(f)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.

Proof.

(
a
) Take
f=r\times\id_{L}
for an orientation-reversing diffeomorphism
r\colon S^{1}\rightarrow S^{1}
.

(
b
) Let
S^{2k-1}=\{(z_{1},\ldots,z_{k})\mid\sum|z_{j}|^{2}=1\}\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{k}
. Recall that
L=L_{2k-1}(m;q_{1},\ldots,q_{k})
is the quotient of
S^{2k-1}
by the
C_{m}
-action generated by
(z_{1},\ldots,z_{k})\mapsto(\zeta^{q_{1}}z_{1},\ldots,\zeta^{q_{k}}z_{k})
, where
\zeta=e^{2\pi i/m}
. Define
\phi\colon S^{1}\to\Diff(L)
by
e^{2\pi it}\mapsto([z_{1},\ldots,z_{k}]\mapsto[\zeta^{q_{1}t}z_{1},\ldots,\zeta^{q_{k}t}z_{k}])
. Then we can define a suitable diffeomorphism
f
by
f(x,y)=(x,\phi(x)(y))
. ∎

Lemma 6.3
.

For every homotopy automorphism
f\colon S^{1}\times L\rightarrow S^{1}\times L
there is a homotopy automorphism
\bar{f}_{2}\colon L\rightarrow L
such that
f\circ i_{2}\simeq i_{2}\circ\bar{f}_{2}\colon L\rightarrow S^{1}\times L
.

Proof.

Since
\pi_{1}(L)\cong C_{m}
is the torsion subgroup of
\pi_{1}(S^{1}\times L)\cong C_{\infty}\times C_{m}
, the restriction of
\pi_{1}(f)
is an isomorphism
\pi_{1}(L)\rightarrow\pi_{1}(L)
, and hence
\im\pi_{1}(f\circ i_{2})=\im\pi_{1}(\rho)
where the covering
\rho\colon\mathbb{R}\times L\rightarrow S^{1}\times L
is the product of the universal covering of
S^{1}
and
\id_{L}
. By the lifting criterion
[
25
, Proposition 1.33]
,
f\circ i_{2}
has a lift
\widetilde{f}_{2}\colon L\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\times L
such that
f\circ i_{2}=\rho\circ\widetilde{f}_{2}
. Let
\pi\colon\mathbb{R}\times L\rightarrow L
denote the projection. Since
\mathbb{R}
is contractible, the universal covering map
\mathbb{R}\to S^{1}
is null-homotopic. It follows that
\rho\simeq i_{2}\circ\pi
. Thus

f\circ i_{2}=\rho\circ\widetilde{f}_{2}\simeq i_{2}\circ\pi\circ\widetilde{f}_{2}=i_{2}\circ\bar{f}_{2},

where by definition
\bar{f}_{2}=\pi\circ\widetilde{f}_{2}\colon L\rightarrow L
.

This implies that
\pi_{1}(\bar{f}_{2})
is the restriction of
\pi_{1}(f)
, so it is an isomorphism. Since
\pi_{j}(i_{2})
is an isomorphism for
j>1
and
f
is a homotopy automorphism (hence
\pi_{j}(f)
is an isomorphism),
\pi_{j}(\bar{f}_{2})
is also an isomorphism. Therefore
\bar{f}_{2}
is a homotopy automorphism too.
∎

Definition 6.4
.

Let
A_{2k}(m)\leq\Aut(G)
denote the subgroup of matrices
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
0&c\end{smallmatrix}\right)
such that
c^{k}\equiv\pm 1
mod
m
.

Theorem 6.5
.

We have
\im(\pi_{1}\colon\hAut(S^{1}\times L)\rightarrow\Aut(G))=A_{2k}(m)
.

Proof.

Suppose that
f\colon S^{1}\times L\rightarrow S^{1}\times L
is a homotopy automorphism with
\pi_{1}(f)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
0&c\end{smallmatrix}\right)
. By
Lemma

6.3
there is a homotopy automorphism
\bar{f}_{2}\colon L\rightarrow L
such that
f\circ i_{2}\simeq i_{2}\circ\bar{f}_{2}
. This implies that
\pi_{1}(\bar{f}_{2})=c
, so by
Lemma

6.1

c^{k}\equiv\pm 1
mod
m
. Therefore
\pi_{1}(f)\in A_{2k}(m)
.

Let
c\in C_{m}^{\times}
be such that
c^{k}\equiv\pm 1
mod
m
. By
Lemma

6.1
there is a homotopy automorphism
f\colon L\rightarrow L
such that
\pi_{1}(f)=c
. Then
\id_{S^{1}}\times f
is a homotopy automorphism of
S^{1}\times L
such that
\pi_{1}(\id_{S^{1}}\times f)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&c\end{smallmatrix}\right)
. Matrices of this form, together with the matrices realised in
Lemma

6.2
, generate
A_{2k}(m)
. Therefore
A_{2k}(m)\subseteq\im(\pi_{1}\colon\hAut(S^{1}\times L)\rightarrow\Aut(G))
.
∎

6.2.
The Whitehead torsion

Next we prove that every homotopy automorphism of
S^{1}\times L
is simple.

Theorem 6.6
.

If
f\in\hAut(S^{1}\times L)
, then
\tau(f)=0
.

Remark 6.7
.

When
m
is odd, this also follows from results of Khan and Hsiang-Jahren. By
[
35
, Corollary 6.2]
,
\hAut(S^{1}\times L)
is generated by maps of the form
f\times\id_{L}
,
\id_{S^{1}}\times g
and
r_{h}
, where
f\in\hAut(S^{1})
,
g\in\hAut(L)
,
h\colon S^{1}\rightarrow\Map(L)
and
r_{h}(x,y)=(x,h(x)(y))
. By
Proposition

2.32
, generators of the first two types have vanishing Whitehead torsion. If
m
is odd, then it follows from
[
28
, Proposition 3.1]
that
\tau(r_{h})=0
(see also
[
35
, Corollary 6.4]
).

The proof consists of two parts, which we will prove in the following two lemmas. For a CW complex
X
and an integer
n\geq 0
, let
\sk_{n}(X)
denote the
n
-skeleton of
X
. We will endow
S^{1}\times L
with a standard CW decomposition, that we recall during the next proof.

Lemma 6.8
.

If
g\colon S^{1}\times L\rightarrow S^{1}\times L
is a homotopy automorphism such that
g\big|_{L\cup\sk_{2k-2}(S^{1}\times L)}=\id
, then
\tau(g)=0
.

Proof.

We will denote the generators of the components of
G
by
\alpha\in C_{\infty}
and
\beta\in C_{m}
. Let
\Sigma:=\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}\beta^{j}\in\mathbb{Z}C_{m}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}G
be the norm element of
\mathbb{Z}C_{m}
.

The lens space
L=L_{2k-1}(m;q_{1},\ldots,q_{k})
has a CW decomposition with one cell
E_{j}
in each dimension
0\leq j\leq 2k-1
(see e.g.
[
15
, §28]
). The corresponding cellular chain complex
(C^{L}_{j},d^{L}_{j})
with
\mathbb{Z}C_{m}
coefficients has
C^{L}_{j}\cong\mathbb{Z}C_{m}
for every
j
and

d^{L}_{j}=\begin{cases}\Sigma&\text{if $j$ is even}\\
\beta^{r_{h}}-1&\text{if $j=2h-1$}\end{cases}

where
r_{h}q_{h}\equiv 1
mod
m
.

The product
M=S^{1}\times L
has a CW decomposition containing the cells of
L
, plus a
(j+1)
-cell
E^{\prime}_{j+1}
, the product of
E_{j}
and the
1
-cell of
S^{1}
, for every
0\leq j\leq 2k-1
. Its chain complex
(C^{M}_{j},d^{M}_{j})
with
\mathbb{Z}G
coefficients is the tensor product of
(C^{L}_{j},d^{L}_{j})
and the chain complex of
S^{1}
with
\mathbb{Z}C_{\infty}
coefficients,
\mathbb{Z}C_{\infty}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha-1}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbb{Z}C_{\infty}
. It has
C^{M}_{0}\cong C^{M}_{2k}\cong\mathbb{Z}G
and
C^{M}_{j}\cong\mathbb{Z}G^{2}
for every
1\leq j\leq 2k-1
(with
\mathbb{Z}G\oplus\left\{0\right\}
corresponding to
E_{j}
and
\left\{0\right\}\oplus\mathbb{Z}G
corresponding to
E^{\prime}_{j}
). The differentials are

\begin{gathered}d^{M}_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}\beta^{r_{1}}-1\\
\alpha-1\end{pmatrix},\quad d^{M}_{2h-1}=\begin{pmatrix}\beta^{r_{h}}-1&0\\
\alpha-1&\Sigma\end{pmatrix}\quad\text{for $h>1$},\\
d^{M}_{2k}=(1-\alpha,\beta^{r_{k}}-1),\quad d^{M}_{2h}=\begin{pmatrix}\Sigma&0\\
1-\alpha&\beta^{r_{h}}-1\end{pmatrix}\quad\text{for $h<k$}.\end{gathered}

We write elements of
\mathbb{Z}G^{j}
as row vectors, so a homomorphism
\mathbb{Z}G^{j}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}G^{h}
is multiplication on the right by a
j\times h
matrix.

We can assume that
g
is cellular, let
g_{j}\colon C^{M}_{j}\rightarrow C^{M}_{j}
denote the induced chain map. Since
g\big|_{L\cup\sk_{2k-2}(L\times S^{1})}=\id
, we have
g_{j}=\id
for every
j\leq 2k-2
and
g_{2k-1}\big|_{\mathbb{Z}G\oplus\left\{0\right\}}=\id
.

We investigate the map
g_{2k-1}\colon C_{2k-1}^{M}\to C_{2k-1}^{M}
. It is represented by a
2\times 2
matrix with entries in
\mathbb{Z}G
, with respect to the basis corresponding to
E_{2k-1}
and
E_{2k-1}^{\prime}
. Since
g_{2k-1}\big|_{\mathbb{Z}G\oplus\left\{0\right\}}=\id
, the matrix is
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
x&y\end{smallmatrix}\right)
for some
x,y\in\mathbb{Z}G
. To compute
x
and
y
, we consider the difference between
g
and
\Id
on
E_{2k-1}^{\prime}
.
Since
g\big|_{\partial E^{\prime}_{2k-1}}=\id
, the cell

E^{\prime}_{2k-1}
and
g(E^{\prime}_{2k-1})
together form a map
S^{2k-1}\rightarrow S^{1}\times L
. This map represents an element of
\pi_{2k-1}(S^{1}\times L)\cong\pi_{2k-1}(\mathbb{R}\times S^{2k-1})\cong\mathbb{Z}
, so its homotopy class is an integer multiple of that of the inclusion
S^{2k-1}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\times S^{2k-1}
, composed with the projection
\mathbb{R}\times S^{2k-1}\rightarrow S^{1}\times L
. The image of the fundamental class of
S^{2k-1}
under this composition is represented by the chain
\Sigma\in\mathbb{Z}G\oplus\left\{0\right\}<\mathbb{Z}G^{2}\cong C^{M}_{2k-1}
. So
E^{\prime}_{2k-1}
and
g(E^{\prime}_{2k-1})
differ by some integer multiple of
\Sigma\in\mathbb{Z}G\oplus\left\{0\right\}
.
Therefore (by changing
g
on
E^{\prime}_{2k-1}
and
E^{\prime}_{2k}
, to replace it with another cellular map which is homotopic to it) we can assume that
y=1
and
x=a\Sigma
for some
a\in\mathbb{Z}
. That is,
g_{2k-1}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
a\Sigma&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.

Since
(g_{j})
is a chain map, we have
d^{M}_{2k}\circ g_{2k}=g_{2k-1}\circ d^{M}_{2k}
.
When converted to matrices this yields

g_{2k}(1-\alpha,\beta^{r_{k}}-1)=(1-\alpha,\beta^{r_{k}}-1)\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
a\Sigma&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)=(1-\alpha,\beta^{r_{k}}-1),

since
\beta^{r_{k}}\Sigma=\Sigma
.
Looking at the first coordinate, we have
g_{2k}(1-\alpha)=(1-\alpha)
. Multiplication by
(1-\alpha)
is an injective map
\mathbb{Z}G\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}G
, so we deduce that
g_{2k}=1
.

Therefore
g_{j}
is an isomorphism for every
j
. By
Lemma

2.18
we can compute the torsion via the formula
\tau(g)=\sum_{j=0}^{2k}(-1)^{j}[g_{j}]
. Since
g_{2k-1}
is an elementary matrix and
g_{j}=\id
for
j\neq 2k-1
, this implies that
\tau(g)=0
.
∎

Lemma 6.9
.

For every homotopy automorphism
f\colon S^{1}\times L\rightarrow S^{1}\times L
there is another homotopy automorphism
g
such that
\tau(f)=\tau(g)
and
g\big|_{L\cup\sk_{2k-2}(L\times S^{1})}=\id
.

Proof.

By
Lemma

6.3
there is a homotopy automorphism
\bar{f}_{2}\colon L\rightarrow L
such that
f\circ i_{2}\simeq i_{2}\circ\bar{f}_{2}
. Let
\overline{h}\colon L\rightarrow L
be the homotopy inverse of
\bar{f}_{2}
, and let
h:=\id_{S^{1}}\times\overline{h}\colon S^{1}\times L\rightarrow S^{1}\times L
. Note that
h\circ i_{2}=i_{2}\circ\overline{h}\colon L\to S^{1}\times L
. By
Corollary

2.33

\tau(h)=0
. Let
f^{\prime}=f\circ h
. Then by
Proposition

2.31
, we have that
\tau(f^{\prime})=\tau(f)
. Moreover, combining the above observations we have

f^{\prime}\circ i_{2}=f\circ h\circ i_{2}=f\circ i_{2}\circ\overline{h}\simeq i_{2}\circ\bar{f}_{2}\circ\overline{h}\simeq i_{2}.

This means that after changing
f^{\prime}
by a homotopy we can assume that
f^{\prime}\big|_{L}=\id
.

The embedding
i_{1}\colon S^{1}\rightarrow S^{1}\times L
represents
(1,0)\in\pi_{1}(S^{1}\times L)\cong C_{\infty}\times C_{m}
. Since
\pi_{1}(f^{\prime})
is an automorphism of
C_{\infty}\times C_{m}
,
\pi_{1}(f^{\prime})^{-1}(1,0)=(a,b)
for some
a\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}
,
b\in C_{m}
. It follows from
Lemma

6.2
that there is a diffeomorphism
h\in\Diff(S^{1}\times L)
such that
\pi_{1}(h)(1,0)=(a,b)
. Let
f^{\prime\prime}=f^{\prime}\circ h
. Then
\tau(f^{\prime\prime})=\tau(f^{\prime})
by
Proposition

2.31
and Chapman’s
Theorem

2.6
.
Moreover
\pi_{1}(f^{\prime\prime})(1,0)=(1,0)
, i.e.
f^{\prime\prime}\circ i_{1}\simeq i_{1}
. The diffeomorphisms constructed in
Lemma

6.2
keep
L
pointwise fixed, so
f^{\prime\prime}\big|_{L}=\id
, and after applying a homotopy we can also assume
f^{\prime\prime}\big|_{S^{1}}=\id
.

Finally, we recursively construct maps
f^{\prime\prime}_{j}
for every
1\leq j\leq 2k-2
such that
f^{\prime\prime}\simeq f^{\prime\prime}_{j}
rel
S^{1}\vee L
and
f^{\prime\prime}_{j}\big|_{L\cup\sk_{j}(L\times S^{1})}=\id
. We start with
f^{\prime\prime}_{1}=f^{\prime\prime}
. If
f^{\prime\prime}_{j-1}
is already defined, we consider the single
j
-cell
E^{\prime}_{j}
in
S^{1}\times L-L
. Since
f^{\prime\prime}_{j-1}\big|_{\partial E^{\prime}_{j}}=\id
, the cell
E^{\prime}_{j}
and
f^{\prime\prime}_{j-1}(E^{\prime}_{j})
together form a map
S^{j}\rightarrow S^{1}\times L
. This map is nullhomotopic (because
\pi_{j}(S^{1}\times L)\cong\pi_{j}(\mathbb{R}\times S^{2k-1})=0
for
2\leq j\leq 2k-2
), so
f^{\prime\prime}_{j-1}\big|_{E^{\prime}_{j}}
is homotopic to
\id_{E^{\prime}_{j}}
rel
\partial E^{\prime}_{j}
. We can extend this homotopy to a homotopy between
f^{\prime\prime}_{j-1}
and an
f^{\prime\prime}_{j}
with
f^{\prime\prime}_{j}\big|_{L\cup\sk_{j}(L\times S^{1})}=\id
. Therefore
f^{\prime\prime}_{j}
can be defined for every
1\leq j\leq 2k-2
.

To complete the proof of
Lemma

6.9
, we take
g=f^{\prime\prime}_{2k-2}\colon S^{1}\times L\to S^{1}\times L
.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

6.6
.

Consider a homotopy equivalence
f\colon S^{1}\times L\to S^{1}\times L
. By
Lemma

6.9
, we can replace
f
by a homotopy equivalence
g\colon S^{1}\times L\to S^{1}\times L
such that
\tau(f)=\tau(g)
and
g\big|_{L\cup\sk_{2k-2}(L\times S^{1})}=\id
. Then
g
satisfies the hypotheses of
Lemma

6.8
, and so
\tau(g)=0
. Therefore
\tau(f)=0
, and
f
is a simple homotopy equivalence, as desired.
∎

7.
The proof of
B
,
C
, and
D

We can now combine the results of the previous sections to prove
B
,
C
, and
D
. Let
n=2k\geq 4
be an even integer and fix a category
\CAT
satisfying
1.1
.

Recall the definition of the sets
\mathcal{M}^{h}_{s}(M)
,
\mathcal{M}^{\hCob}_{s}(M)
and
\mathcal{M}^{h}_{s,\hCob}(M)
from
Definition

4.10
. Also recall the definition of
A_{2k}(m)
(
Definition

6.4
):

A_{2k}(m)\leq\Aut(C_{\infty}\times C_{m})
denotes the subgroup of matrices
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
0&c\end{smallmatrix}\right)
such that
c^{k}\equiv\pm 1
mod
m
.
Note that
A_{2k}(m)
acts on
\Wh(C_{\infty}\times C_{m})
and on the subgroups
\mathcal{I}_{n}(C_{\infty}\times C_{m})
and
\mathcal{J}_{n}(C_{\infty}\times C_{m})
.

Proposition 7.1
.

Let
m\geq 2
and
q_{1},\ldots,q_{k}
such that
\gcd(m,q_{j})=1
. Let
L=L_{2k-1}(m;q_{1},\ldots,q_{k})
be an
(n-1)
-dimensional lens space with
\pi_{1}(L)\cong C_{m}
. Let
G:=C_{\infty}\times C_{m}\cong\pi_{1}(S^{1}\times L)
. Then there are bijections of pointed sets
\mathcal{M}^{h}_{s}(S^{1}\times L)\cong\mathcal{J}_{n}(G)/A_{2k}(m)
,
\mathcal{M}^{\hCob}_{s}(S^{1}\times L)\cong\mathcal{I}_{n}(G)/A_{2k}(m)
and
\mathcal{M}^{h}_{s,\hCob}(S^{1}\times L)\cong\widehat{H}^{n+1}(C_{2};\Wh(G))/A_{2k}(m)
.

Proof.

Firstly note that, since
C_{\infty}\times C_{m}
is polycyclic, it is a good group
[
19
,
36
]
, so
1.1
is satisfied. Moreover, since the map
\psi
is surjective for
G=C_{\infty}\times C_{m}
,
w\equiv 1
and
n
even by
Proposition

3.12
, the vertical maps in the diagram of
Theorem

4.11
(
c
) are bijections if
M=S^{1}\times L
. Therefore
\mathcal{M}^{h}_{s}(S^{1}\times L)\cong\mathcal{J}_{n}(G)/\hAut(S^{1}\times L)
,
\mathcal{M}^{\hCob}_{s}(S^{1}\times L)\cong q(\mathcal{I}_{n}(G))
, and
\mathcal{M}^{h}_{s,\hCob}(S^{1}\times L)\cong\widehat{H}^{n+1}(C_{2};\Wh(G))/\hAut(S^{1}\times L)
.

By
Theorem

6.6
, every homotopy automorphism of
S^{1}\times L
is simple, therefore the action of
\hAut(S^{1}\times L)
on
\Wh(G)
factors through the action of
\Aut(G)
(see
Definition

4.3
and
Remark

4.18
). By
Theorem

6.5
, the image of
\pi_{1}\colon\hAut(S^{1}\times L)\rightarrow\Aut(G)
is
A_{2k}(m)
. In particular, the orbits of the action of
\hAut(S^{1}\times L)
and
A_{2k}(m)
on
\mathcal{J}_{n}(G)
,
\mathcal{I}_{n}(G)
, and
\widehat{H}^{n+1}(C_{2};\Wh(G))
coincide.
This implies that
\mathcal{J}_{n}(G)/\hAut(S^{1}\times L)=\mathcal{J}_{n}(G)/A_{2k}(m)
,
q(\mathcal{I}_{n}(G))=\mathcal{I}_{n}(G)/A_{2k}(m)
. Hence, as required, we have
\widehat{H}^{n+1}(C_{2};\Wh(G))/\hAut(S^{1}\times L)=\widehat{H}^{n+1}(C_{2};\Wh(G))/A_{2k}(m)
.
∎

In particular
Proposition

7.1
implies that

|\mathcal{M}^{h}_{s}(S^{1}\times L)|
,
|\mathcal{M}^{\hCob}_{s}(S^{1}\times L)|
and
|\mathcal{M}^{h}_{s,\hCob}(S^{1}\times L)|
are independent of the choice of the
q_{j}
and of
\CAT
, and only depend on
n
and
m
. This proves part (
a
) of
B
,
C
, and
D
.

From now on, we will write
M^{n}_{m}
for
S^{1}\times L
, where
L
is any
(n-1)
-dimensional lens space with
\pi_{1}(L)\cong C_{m}
.

Lemma 7.2
.

Let
n=2k\geq 4
be an even integer,
m\geq 2
and
G=C_{\infty}\times C_{m}
. Then the following hold.

(a)

|\mathcal{M}^{h}_{s}(M^{n}_{m})|=1
if and only if
\mathcal{J}_{n}(G)=0
.

(b)

|\mathcal{M}^{h}_{s}(M^{n}_{m})|=\infty
if and only if
|\mathcal{J}_{n}(G)|=\infty
.

(c)

If
\mathcal{M}^{h}_{s}(M^{n}_{m})
is finite, then
\frac{|\mathcal{J}_{n}(G)|}{2m^{2}}<|\mathcal{M}^{h}_{s}(M^{n}_{m})|\leq|\mathcal{J}_{n}(G)|
.

(d)

|\mathcal{M}^{\hCob}_{s}(M^{n}_{m})|=1
if and only if
\mathcal{I}_{n}(G)=0
.

(e)

|\mathcal{M}^{\hCob}_{s}(M^{n}_{m})|=\infty
if and only if
|\mathcal{I}_{n}(G)|=\infty
.

(f)

If
\mathcal{M}^{\hCob}_{s}(M^{n}_{m})
is finite, then
\frac{|\mathcal{I}_{n}(G)|}{2m^{2}}<|\mathcal{M}^{\hCob}_{s}(M^{n}_{m})|\leq|\mathcal{I}_{n}(G)|
.

(g)

|\mathcal{M}^{h}_{s,\hCob}(M^{n}_{m})|=1
if and only if
\widehat{H}^{n+1}(C_{2};\Wh(G))=0
.

(h)

|\mathcal{M}^{h}_{s,\hCob}(M^{n}_{m})|=\infty
if and only if
|\widehat{H}^{n+1}(C_{2};\Wh(G))|=\infty
.

(i)

If
\mathcal{M}^{h}_{s,\hCob}(M^{n}_{m})
is finite, then
\frac{|\widehat{H}^{n+1}(C_{2};\Wh(G))|}{2m^{2}}<|\mathcal{M}^{h}_{s,\hCob}(M^{n}_{m})|\leq|\widehat{H}^{n+1}(C_{2};\Wh(G))|
.

Proof.

(
a
) By
Proposition

7.1
,
|\mathcal{M}^{h}_{s}(M^{n}_{m})|=|\mathcal{J}_{n}(G)/A_{2k}(m)|
. The group
A_{2k}(m)\leq\Aut(G)
acts on
\Wh(G)
, and hence on
\mathcal{J}_{n}(G)
, by automorphisms. So
0\in\mathcal{J}_{n}(G)
is a fixed point of this action, and
|\mathcal{J}_{n}(G)/A_{2k}(m)|=1
if and only if
\mathcal{J}_{n}(G)=0
.

(
b
)
\Aut(G)
, and hence its subgroup
A_{2k}(m)
, is finite. Hence
|\mathcal{J}_{n}(G)/A_{2k}(m)|=\infty
if and only if
|\mathcal{J}_{n}(G)|=\infty
.

(
c
) It is easy to see that
\frac{|\mathcal{J}_{n}(G)|}{|A_{2k}(m)|}\leq|\mathcal{J}_{n}(G)/A_{2k}(m)|\leq|\mathcal{J}_{n}(G)|
. We have
|A_{2k}(m)|\leq|\Aut(G)|\leq 2m(m-1)<2m^{2}
since elements of
\Aut(G)
can be represented as matrices of the form
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
0&c\end{smallmatrix}\right)
, where
a\in\{\pm 1\}
,
b\in C_{m}
,
c\in C_{m}^{\times}
.

The proofs of parts
(
d
),
(
e
), and
(
f
) (resp. parts (
g
),
(
h
) and
(
i
)) are entirely analogous to those of parts
(
a
),
(
b
), and
(
c
) respectively, and so will be omitted for brevity.
∎

Theorem 7.3
.

Let
n=2k\geq 4
be an even integer and
m\geq 2
. Then the following hold.

(a)

|\mathcal{M}^{h}_{s}(M^{n}_{m})|=1
if and only if
m\in\{2,3,5,6,7,10,11,13,14,17,19\}
.

(b)

|\mathcal{M}^{h}_{s}(M^{n}_{m})|=\infty
if and only if
m
is not square-free.

(c)

|\mathcal{M}^{h}_{s}(M^{n}_{m})|\to\infty
as
m\to\infty
(uniformly in
n
).

(d)

|\mathcal{M}^{\hCob}_{s}(M^{n}_{m})|=1
if and only if
m\in\{2,3,5,6,7,10,11,13,14,15,17,19,29\}
.

(e)

|\mathcal{M}^{\hCob}_{s}(M^{n}_{m})|=\infty
if and only if
m
is not square-free.

(f)

|\mathcal{M}^{\hCob}_{s}(M^{n}_{m})|\to\infty
as
m\to\infty
(uniformly in
n
).

(g)

There are infinitely many
m
such that
|\mathcal{M}^{h}_{s,\hCob}(M^{n}_{m})|=1
for every
n
.

(h)

|\mathcal{M}^{h}_{s,\hCob}(M^{n}_{m})|
is finite for every
n
and
m
.

(i)

\displaystyle\limsup_{m\to\infty}\Bigl(\inf_{n}|\mathcal{M}^{h}_{s,\hCob}(M^{n}_{m})|\Bigr)=\infty
.

Proof.

(
a
), (
b
), (
d
), (
e
), (
g
), and (
h
) follow immediately from
Lemma

7.2
and
Theorems

5.14
,
5.15
, and
5.17
.

(
c
) For every
m
, it follows from
Lemma

7.2
(
c
) that
|\mathcal{M}^{h}_{s}(M^{n}_{m})|>\frac{|\mathcal{J}_{n}(C_{\infty}\times C_{m})|}{2m^{2}}
for every
n
. By
Theorem

5.14
(
iii
)
\frac{|\mathcal{J}_{n}(C_{\infty}\times C_{m})|}{2m^{2}}\to\infty
as
m\to\infty
. Item (
f
), can be proved similarly using
Theorem

5.15
(
iii
).

(
i
) For every
m
, it follows from
Lemma

7.2
(
i
) that
\inf_{n}|\mathcal{M}^{h}_{s,\hCob}(M^{n}_{m})|>\frac{|\widehat{H}^{n+1}(C_{2};\Wh(C_{\infty}\times C_{m}))|}{2m^{2}}
. By
Theorem

5.17
(
iii
), we see that
\frac{1}{2m^{2}}\sup_{l\leq m}|\widehat{H}^{n+1}(C_{2};\Wh(C_{\infty}\times C_{l}))|\to\infty
as
m\to\infty
. This implies that

\sup_{l\leq m}\frac{|\widehat{H}^{n+1}(C_{2};\Wh(C_{\infty}\times C_{l}))|}{2l^{2}}\to\infty
as
m\to\infty
.
So the map
m\mapsto\frac{|\widehat{H}^{n+1}(C_{2};\Wh(C_{\infty}\times C_{m}))|}{2m^{2}}
is unbounded. Therefore
\limsup_{m\to\infty}\frac{|\widehat{H}^{n+1}(C_{2};\Wh(C_{\infty}\times C_{m}))|}{2m^{2}}=\infty
.
∎

This completes the proof of the remaining parts of
B
,
C
, and
D
, subject to the proofs of
Theorems

11.1
,
11.2
, and
11.3
which will be postponed until
Section

11
. We can now deduce:

Theorem 7.4
.

There exists an infinite collection of orientable,
\CAT

n
-manifolds that are all homotopy equivalent to one another but are pairwise not simple homotopy equivalent.

Proof.

By
Theorem

7.3
(
b
), if
m
is not square-free (e.g. if
m=4
), then
\mathcal{M}^{h}_{s}(M^{n}_{m})
is infinite. Note that
M^{n}_{m}=S^{1}\times L
is orientable, so the same is true for every
n
-manifold homotopy equivalent to it. Therefore we can get a suitable infinite collection by choosing a representative from each element of
\mathcal{M}^{h}_{s}(M^{n}_{m})
.
∎

This completes the proof of
A
. Note that this does not depend on the results postponed to
Section

11
. In particular, if
m
is not square-free, then
|NK_{1}(\mathbb{Z}C_{m})|=\infty
(
Theorem

1.5
) which implies
|\mathcal{J}_{n}(C_{\infty}\times C_{m})|=\infty
(
Lemma

5.12
) and so
|\mathcal{M}^{h}_{s}(M^{n}_{m})|=\infty
(
Lemma

7.2
(
b
)).

Part III
The involution on
\widetilde{K}_{0}(\mathbb{Z}C_{m})

The aim of this part will be to prove
Theorems

11.1
,
11.2
, and
11.3
, which are key ingredients in the proofs of
Theorems

5.14
,
5.15
, and
5.17
respectively. In
Sections

8
and
9
, we will recall the necessary background on class groups, Tate cohomology, and
\mathbb{Z}C_{2}
-modules. The main technical heart of this part will be
Section

10
where we will investigate the involution on
\widetilde{K}_{0}(\mathbb{Z}C_{m})
and prove general results which allow it to be computed. In
Section

11
, we make use of the results in
Section

10
to prove
Theorems

11.1
,
11.2
, and
11.3
. Throughout, we assume that all modules are left modules.

8.
Locally free class groups

We will now recall the theory of locally free class groups for orders in semisimple
\mathbb{Q}
-algebras. Good references for this material are
[
67
, Section 1-3]
and
[
16
, Section 49A & 50E]
.

8.1.
Definitions and properties

Recall that for a ring
A
, a nonzero
A
-module is
simple
if it contains no simple
A
-submodules other than itself and
0
, and is
semisimple
if isomorphic as an
A
-module to a direct sum of its simple
A
-submodules.
We say that a ring
A
is
simple
(resp.
semisimple
) if
A
, viewed as an
A
-module, is simple (resp. semisimple). For a field
K
, a
K
-algebra
is a ring
A
for which
K
is a subring of the centre
Z(A)
. A
K
-algebra is
finite-dimensional
if it is finite-dimensional as a
K
-vector space.

Let
A
be a finite-dimensional semisimple
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra. An
order
in
A
is a subring
\Lambda\subseteq A
that is finitely generated as an abelian group and which has
\mathbb{Q}\cdot\Lambda=A
.
For example, let
G
be a finite group. Then
A=\mathbb{Q}G
is a finite dimensional
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra which is semisimple by Maschke’s theorem in representation theory, and
\Lambda=\mathbb{Z}G
is an order in
A
.

From now on, fix an order
\Lambda
in a finite-dimensional semisimple
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra
A
. For a prime
p
, let
\Lambda_{p}=\mathbb{Z}_{p}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\Lambda
and let
A_{p}=\mathbb{Q}_{p}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}A
denote the
p
-adic completions of
\Lambda
and
A
.

Definition 8.1
.

A
\Lambda
-module
M
is
locally free
if
M_{p}=\mathbb{Z}_{p}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}M
is a free
\Lambda_{p}
-module for all primes
p
.

The following is
[
67
, Lemma 2.1]
. Note that the converse need not hold, i.e. there exist orders
\Lambda
and projective
\Lambda
-modules that are not locally free
[
67
, p. 156]
.

Proposition 8.2
.

If
M
is a locally free
\Lambda
-module, then
M
is projective.

We say that two locally free
\Lambda
-modules
M
and
N
are
stably isomorphic
, written
M\cong_{\st}N
, if there exists
r,s\geq 0
such that
M\oplus\Lambda^{r}\cong N\oplus\Lambda^{s}
are isomorphic as
\Lambda
-modules.

Definition 8.3
.

Define the
locally free class group

C(\Lambda)
to be the set of equivalence classes of locally free
\Lambda
-modules up to stable isomorphism. This is an abelian group under direct sum (since the direct sum of locally free modules is locally free).

It follows that
C(\Lambda)\leq\widetilde{K}_{0}(\Lambda)
is a subgroup, where
\widetilde{K}_{0}
is the
0
th reduced algebraic
K
-group as defined in
Section

5.1
. It is a consequence of the Jordan-Zassenhaus theorem that
C(\Lambda)
is finite
[
16
, Remark 49.11 (ii)]
.

We will now specialise to the case where
\Lambda=\mathbb{Z}G
for
G
a finite group. In contrast to the situation for general orders, we have the following
[
67
, p. 156]
. By
Proposition

8.2
, this implies that a
\mathbb{Z}G
-module is projective if and only if it is locally free.

Proposition 8.4
.

Let
G
be a finite group.

(i)

If
M
is a projective
\mathbb{Z}G
-module, then
M
is locally free.

(ii)

There is an isomorphism of abelian groups
\widetilde{K}_{0}(\mathbb{Z}G)\cong C(\mathbb{Z}G)
. In particular,
\widetilde{K}_{0}(\mathbb{Z}G)
is finite.

Finally we note the following which relates locally free class groups to ideal class groups (see
[
56
, Section 35]
).

Proposition 8.5
.

Let
K/\mathbb{Q}
be a finite field extension. Then
C(\mathcal{O}_{K})
coincides with the ideal class group of
\mathcal{O}_{K}
.

8.2.
Kernel groups

Let
A
be a finite-dimensional semisimple
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra
A
. An order in
A
is said to be
maximal
if it is not properly contained in another other order in
A
. Since every finite field extension of
\mathbb{Q}
is separable,
A
is a separable algebra and so every order in
A
is contained in a maximal order
[
66
, Proposition 5.1]
.

Let
\Lambda
be an order in
A
and let
\Gamma
be a maximal order in
A
containing
\Lambda
. The inclusion map
i\colon\Lambda\hookrightarrow\Gamma
induces a map
i_{*}\colon C(\Lambda)\to C(\Gamma)
given by extension of scalars
[M]\mapsto[\Gamma\otimes_{\Lambda}M]
which is necessarily surjective by
[
16
, Theorem 49.25]
.

Definition 8.6
.

Define the
kernel group

D(\Lambda)
to be the kernel of the map
i_{*}\colon C(\Lambda)\to C(\Gamma)
. This is often also referred to as the defect group.

The group
C(\Gamma)
does not depend on the choice of maximal order in
A
; in fact, if
\Gamma_{1}
,
\Gamma_{2}
are maximal orders in
A
, then
C(\Gamma_{1})\cong C(\Gamma_{2})

[
16
, Theorem 49.32]
. Furthermore, the kernel group
D(\Lambda)
does not depend on the choice of maximal order. This can be seen from the fact that it can be defined without reference to a maximal order: if
M
is a locally free
\Lambda
-module, then
[M]\in D(\Lambda)
if and only if there exists a finitely generated
\Lambda
-module
X
such that
M\oplus X\cong\Lambda^{n}\oplus X
for some
n

[
16
, Proposition 49.34]
.

In particular, we have a well-defined exact sequence of abelian groups:

0\to D(\Lambda)\to C(\Lambda)\to C(\Gamma)\to 0

where
\Gamma
can be taken to be any maximal order in
A
containing
\Lambda
.

8.3.
The idèlic approach to locally free class groups

Let
\Lambda
be an order in a finite-dimensional semisimple
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra
A
.

Definition 8.7
.

Define the
idèle group

J(A)=\{(\alpha_{p})\in\prod_{p}A_{p}^{\times}\mid\alpha_{p}\in\Lambda_{p}^{\times}\textit{ for all but finitely many $p$}\}\subseteq\prod_{p}A_{p}^{\times}.

As a subgroup of
A_{p}^{\times}
, this is independent of the choice of order
\Lambda

[
16
, p. 218]
.
Every class in
C(\Lambda)
is represented by a locally free
\Lambda
-module
M\subseteq A

[
16
, p. 218]
. For each
p
, there exists
\alpha_{p}\in A_{p}
such that
M_{p}=\Lambda_{p}\alpha_{p}\subseteq A_{p}
. For all but finitely many
p
,
M_{p}\cong\Lambda_{p}
and so
\alpha_{p}\in\Lambda_{p}^{\times}
. In particular,
\alpha=(\alpha_{p})\in J(A)
. Conversely, given an idèle
\alpha\in J(A)
, we have that
\Lambda\alpha=A\cap\bigcap_{p}\Lambda_{p}\alpha_{p}\subseteq A
is a locally free
\Lambda
-ideal.
Let
\alpha,\beta\in J(A)
. Then
\Lambda\alpha\cong\Lambda\beta
as
\Lambda
-modules if and only if
\beta\in U(\Lambda)\cdot\alpha\cdot A^{\times}
where
A^{\times}\subseteq J(A)
by sending
a\in A^{\times}
to
\alpha_{p}=1\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}a
for all
p
, and
U(\Lambda)=\{(\alpha_{p})\in\prod_{p}A_{p}^{\times}\mid\alpha_{p}\in\Lambda_{p}^{\times}\text{ for all $p$}\}\subseteq J(A)

[
16
, 49.6]
.
Furthermore, we have that
\Lambda\alpha\oplus\Lambda\beta\cong\Lambda\oplus\Lambda\alpha\beta

[
16
, 49.8]
. This leads to the following.

Proposition 8.8
.

There is a surjective group homomorphism

[\Lambda\,\cdot\,]\colon J(A)\twoheadrightarrow C(\Lambda),\quad\alpha\mapsto[\Lambda\alpha].

Remark 8.9
.

Whilst we will not make use of it in this article, we note that this leads to the formula

C(\Lambda)\cong\frac{J(A)}{J_{0}(A)\cdot A^{\times}\cdot U(\Lambda)}

for the locally free class group, where
J_{0}(A)=\{x\in J(A)\mid\nr(x)=1\}
and
\nr\colon J(A)\to J(Z(A))
is induced by the reduced norm. This is due to Fröhlich
[
21
]
(see also
[
16
, Theorem 49.22]
).

8.4.
Involutions on locally free class groups

Let
\Lambda
be an order in a finite-dimensional semisimple
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra
A
. Suppose further that
A
is a ring equipped with an involution
\overline{\cdot}\colon A\to A
which restricts to
\Lambda
, i.e. the map
\overline{\cdot}
is an involution on
A
as an abelian group which satisfies
\overline{xy}=\overline{y}\cdot\overline{x}
for all
x,y\in A
and
\overline{x}\in\Lambda
for all
x\in\Lambda
.
For example, if
G
is a finite group and
w\colon G\to\{\pm 1\}
is an orientation character, then
A=\mathbb{Q}G
has an involution given by
\sum_{i=1}^{k}n_{i}g_{i}\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{k}w(g_{i})n_{i}g_{i}^{-1}
for
n_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
and
g_{i}\in G
which restricts to
\Lambda=\mathbb{Z}G
(see
Section

2.2
). Given a (left)
\Lambda
-module
M
, we now have the notion of a dual (left)
\Lambda
-module
M^{*}
(see
Definition

2.23
).

We now note the following properties of the dual of locally free modules. This follows from the fact that, by
Proposition

8.2
, locally free modules are projective and hence reflexive.

Lemma 8.10
.

Let
M
be a locally free
\Lambda
-module. Then:

(i)

M^{*}
is locally free;

(ii)

The evaluation map
\text{ev}:M\to M^{**}
,
m\mapsto(f\mapsto f(m))
is an isomorphism of
\Lambda
-modules.

We can use this to define an involution on the locally free class group.

Definition 8.11
.

There is an involution of abelian groups:

\ast\colon C(\Lambda)\to C(\Lambda),\quad[M]\mapsto-[M^{*}].

That is,
\ast
is a group homomorphism such that
\ast^{2}=\id_{C(\Lambda)}
.

In the case
\Lambda=\mathbb{Z}G
, we have that
\widetilde{K}_{0}(\mathbb{Z}G)\cong C(\mathbb{Z}G)
and the involution above coincides with the standard involution on
\widetilde{K}_{0}
as defined in
Section

5.1
.
